🇫🇷 FLASH – LFI a dévoilé le programme de Jean-Luc Mélenchon pour la présidentielle de 2027. Parmi les propositions :

– Autoriser jusqu’à 3 jours d’arrêt maladie autodéclaré, sans consulter de médecin.

– Accorder 7 jours de congé après le décès d’un ami.

– Porter le SMIC à 1.700€ net par mois, soit une hausse de 220€.

– Ramener l’âge de départ à la retraite de 64 à 60 ans, avec une retraite à taux plein après 40 années de cotisation.

– Réduire progressivement la durée du travail à 32 heures par semaine.

– Instaurer un service écologique obligatoire de 9 mois pour les moins de 25 ans, rémunéré au SMIC.

– Faire rembourser les soins à 100% par la Sécurité sociale.

– Prendre en charge les séances de psychothérapie des moins de 26 ans.

– Fixer un plafond de 12 millions d’euros pour les héritages.

– Augmenter les APL de 10% pour aider à payer les loyers.

– Bloquer les prix de certains produits essentiels afin de limiter les hausses excessives.

– Revaloriser les salaires des fonctionnaires.

– Garantir un revenu minimum aux jeunes grâce à une allocation.

– Limiter à 1 pour 20 l’écart entre les rémunérations au sein d’une même entreprise.

– Soumettre les aides publiques aux entreprises à des engagements sociaux et écologiques.

– Rétablir l’ISF afin d’accroître l’imposition des grandes fortunes.

– Mettre en place la taxe Zucman pour taxer davantage les milliardaires.

– Répartir l’impôt sur le revenu en 14 tranches pour augmenter la contribution des plus riches.

– Engager une sortie progressive du nucléaire.

– Atteindre une production d’électricité issue à 100% des énergies renouvelables d’ici 2060.

– Suspendre les zones à faibles émissions (ZFE), qui restreignent la circulation des véhicules les plus polluants dans certaines villes.

– Passer de la Ve à la VIe République en donnant davantage de pouvoir aux citoyens.

– Supprimer le 49.3, qui permet au gouvernement de faire adopter un texte sans vote.

– Intensifier la lutte contre les discriminations, notamment au sein de la police.

– Reconnaître comme crime d’État le massacre des Algériens du 17 octobre 1961.

– Renégocier les traités européens et refuser l’application de certaines règles européennes jugées incompatibles avec le programme.

– Quitter progressivement le commandement militaire intégré de l’OTAN.

– Soutenir la création d’un État palestinien aux côtés d’Israël.

DWTS' Jenna Johnson Breaks Bone Night Before Quarter Finals

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Dancing With the Stars pro Jenna Johnson—partnered with Harry Shum Jr. in season 35—revealed that she broke her nose while rehearsing for Grammy Night and how the injury could impact her performance.

Jenna Johnson can smell the finish line—well, at least metaphorically.

As the Dancing With the Stars pro salsaed one step closer to clinching the Mirrorball Trophy with celebrity partner Harry Shum Jr., she revealed that she injured her nose during a rehearsal ahead of season 35's quarter finals.

"So last night I definitely broke my nose during camera blocking," Johnson explained on Instagram Nov. 10, while giving fans a glimpse at her face, noting on the right side it was "a little swollen."

But the ballroom dancer was still in good spirits after the accident, quipping, "No black eyes today, which is awesome." She kept up her sense of humor with the caption, "Salsa: 1, Jenna’s Nose: 0, Happy Show Day."

Johnson, 32, and Shum Jr., 44, were preparing for Grammy Night, during which the couple—who currently hold the highest cumulative score of the season—will perform a salsa to Lady Gaga and Bruno Mars' "Die With A Smile."

As such, Johnson warned ahead of the romantic duet, "If my nose looks just four times the size, that's why."

The two-time champion—who won season 26 with Adam Rippon and season 33 with Joey Graziadei—is taking the unwelcome twist in stride, proving that she is still fierce competition. Johnson's third title would tie her with husband Valentin Chmerkovskiy and Mark Ballas for current pros with the most Mirrorballs. (Derek Hough is the all-time DWTS champion with six wins during his 17 seasons as a pro.)

And Johnson's well aware the competition can come with difficult moments. After a tense exchange with judge Carrie Ann Inaba over a critique of a dance move during Super Bowl Week, she clarified, "This is a competition. There are judges for a reason. They do need to give feedback. So, yeah, we will take it."

Fortunately for Johnson, she has Chmerkovskiy—with whom she shares son Rome, 3—by her side throughout the competition. And the couple aren't the only pros who made rum-ba for romance. Read on to see the DWTS coaches' off-camera dance partners.

Jenna and Val didn't waltz into a relationship right away. When she joined Dancing With the Stars in 2014 and they were paired together for a routine, "We had physical chemistry," he recalled on The Squeeze in 2025. "I liked her energy and spirit. And then throughout the season, we kind of entertained that attraction."After initially trying to resist the sparks, Jenna and Val dated on and off for a few years. They got engaged in 2018 and tied the knot the following year—growing their family with son Rome in 2023."As much as we tried to fight it," Jenna said on The Morning After With Kelly Stratford in 2025, "we were drawn to each other always."

Looking for a new dance partner in 2008, Pasha's coach suggested he team up with Daniella.

Initially hesitant due to their six-and-a-half-year age gap, he recalled to DancesportInfo in 2017, "

After her four-year marriage to DWTS' Sasha Farber ended in 2022, Emma formed a romance with fellow pro Alan. However, the move wasn't a quickstep. While Emma and Alan sparked romance rumors in 2024, they didn't go public with their relationship until they kissed on stage during the 2025 DWTS Tour."We've always been really good friends, and now we have a great relationship," she told E! News in June 2026. "It feels really good."

Scoring a perfect 10 in their love story, Britt and actor Daniel competed together on DWTS in 2022. After they were eliminated, they wondered if their feelings would remain outside of the show and slowly began dating. "I wanted to make sure that it was as real as what we were feeling," Britt explained on The Joe Vulpis Podcast in December 2025, "and it was."

The dancer and the CODA star got engaged in 2023 and tied the knot two years later.

"I love you," Britt told Daniel while signing their vows in a video from their wedding day. "I think my soul loved you before I met you."

Called the Rolls Royce, Adele cruised away from 2026's DWTS: The Next Pro with the win and a new relationship with fellow contestant Allen.

"When I got to know Allen on The Next Pro, I immediately felt that he was one of the kindest souls I’d ever met," she told E! News in October 2026. “Having such a strong bond and friendship first makes our relationship now feel so right.”

While fans have seen Brandon glide across the ballroom, he met Brylee by sliding into her DMs.

"I said, 'Hey, how are you?' he recalled to Joe Vulpis in 2024, "and she ghosted me."

After her mom convinced her to give him a chance, Brylee and Brandon started dating in 2021 and he proposed the following year with a custom movie trailer. Months later, they tied the knot.

"If y’all knew Bry the way I do," Brandon wrote on Instagram in May 2026, "your life would be 100 times better."

"His aunt was my Uber driver in L.A., which was crazy," the dancer told It Girl in September 2026. "I got in her car, was talking to her, and she was like, 'I have a nephew who's playing football at USC. You should reach out to him.'"After Rylee followed the tight-end, later suiting up for Utah's BYU, on Instagram, "He messaged me,” she continued, “and then the rest is history."

"Us, our tribe, our adventures past present and future… it’s all everything I’ve ever wanted."

In a meet-cute that’s music to fans’ ears, Mark and BC told Entertainment Tonight they met in 2012 at a friend's house, where they were both performing for a live-stream event.

Marrying in 2016, the singer and the dancer collaborate both in life—as parents to son Banksi—and in work, teaming up for their band Alexander Jean.

Calling BC’s love and support "unmatched," Mark wrote in a 2025 Instagram post, "You're the best wife & friend a man could ask for."

Arsenal star reveals how Mikel Arteta is 'different to any other manager'

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Your weekly briefing on Arsenal, featuring team news and expert analysis from Matt Verri

I would like to be emailed about offers, event and updates from Evening Standard. Read our privacy notice.

Christos Tzolis has revealed how Arsenal boss Mikel Arteta is different to any other manager he has played under.

The Greek joined the Gunners last summer in a £34million move from Club Brugge, that saw the winger return to England after a spell at Norwich City.

The forward had an underwhelming period at Carrow Road before moving to Belgium, where he lifted the Pro League title last season, which helped propel him to the Emirates Stadium.

He has just returned from a hamstring injury on international duty that kept him out for a month, but he made a return to action in the 1-0 win over Liverpool at Anfield on November 1.

Tzolis has spoken of his admiration for Arteta being a big part of why he wanted to move to north London, and he explained to Novasports how the Spaniard differed from any head coach he had had previously.

"He does a lot of things before the matches, some alert games, in the locker room to see the players' readiness,” he said.

“He always has different ideas before each game and approaches it differently.

"I mean, I haven't seen that with any other coach. In the first talk we had in the summer, he asked me about my life, about my life outside of the game as well, about football of course.

“And I spoke for 45 minutes, for sure, and 10-15 minutes as coach, so he was very open to hearing what I had to say."

UEFA Women's Champions League: FC Bayern beat PSG at Parc des Princes

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

FC Bayern climbed to second place in the UEFA Women's Champions League after a 2-1 win over PSG at Parc des Princes on Tuesday night.

Bayern dominated the play from the get-go, but they struggled to create clear-cut chances. Uncharacteristically, Pernille Harder and Klara Bühl lacked precision with the first couple of opportunities before the captain Magdalena Eriksson failed to hit the target with a free header from a corner-kick.

There was also a lengthy VAR check that ended with Bayern's penalty-kick for a handball overturned because of an offside in the buildup. Before PSG even reached Bayern's box for the first time, Griedge Mbock denied Jovana Demnjanovic a clear goalscoring chance with a brilliant last-ditch tackle.

The Frauen-Bundesliga leaders took the lead a couple of minutes later. PSG's captain Paulina Dudek struggled to stop Harder's dribble in the right flank before the Danish international spotted Linda Dallmann in the box. The cutback from the soon-34-year-old didn't reach the midfielder, but the goalkeeper Katarzyna Kiedrzynek's attempt to block the ball resulted in an own-goal.

Surprisingly, PSG showed attacking intent after conceding the opener. That meant more spaces for Bayern to attack, and they doubled their advantage with a magnificent linkup play between Harder and Bühl after Momoko Tanikawa won possession in the final third.

Bühl, who didn't feature for the club since the matchday 3 draw with Real Madrid, scored her second Champions League goal of the season. Compared to the first-half, there were lots of actions in both boxes right after the restart. Kiedrzynek denied Bühl from scoring her tenth goal of the season with a decent save before her side halved the deficit.

Fellow Polish international Dudek scored from the penalty spot after Dallmann's handball twenty minutes into the second-half. Dallmann scored at the other end ten minutes before time, but it was from an offside position.

Despite their dominant display in the first 45 minutes, Bayern struggled for the majority of the second-half as the hosts looked to repeat what Manchester City did to the German giants in the opening matchday. However, the visitors managed to see out the win to momentarily climb to second place.

FC Bayern have a perfect record away from home as their next game will also be on the road against Austria Vienna before hosting the champions Barcelona in the last matchday. Jose Barcala’s side will face second-placed VfL Wolfsburg on Saturday as they aim to extend their lead in the Frauen-Bundesliga.

SAS Boosts Flights To These 2 Major Delta Hubs For Summer 2027

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

SAS is increasing flights to two major Delta Air Lines hubs as part of an extensive expansion of its summer 2027 schedule. The Scandinavian carrier will increase services from Copenhagen Airport (CPH) to Atlanta and Seattle, strengthening connections with its US partner while adding capacity across a total of seven intercontinental routes.

The November 10 announcement also includes larger Airbus aircraft, more than 20 European and Scandinavian route enhancements, and two new destinations. The expansion follows SAS's transition from Star Alliance to SkyTeam, which has brought it closer to Delta and Air France-KLM.

Hartsfield-Jackson Atlanta International Airport (ATL) will increase from five weekly flights to a daily service from Copenhagen, representing 40% growth, while Seattle rises from four to five weekly flights. Both changes restore previously offered frequencies: SAS launched Atlanta flights in June 2024, shortly before its formal move to SkyTeam, wasting no time connecting into the alliance's busiest global hub. SAS also operates nonstop services to three other Delta hubs: Boston, Los Angeles and New York-JFK.

SAS' partnership with Delta formally began in September 2024, just days after joining the alliance, with a revised codeshare agreement. The expanded agreement covered 150 destinations in North America, as well as around 50 destinations in Northern Europe. The cooperation increased again in April 2025, with both carriers adding more connecting opportunities and reciprocal loyalty benefits. Paul Verhagen, Chief Commercial Officer at SAS, said at the time:

“We are thrilled to continue expanding our partnership with Delta, enhancing travel options between Scandinavia and North America. As a proud new member of SkyTeam Alliance, our collaboration offers great opportunities to provide seamless connectivity and benefits across our global network."

The relationship is expected to deepen further through Air France-KLM's proposed acquisition of a controlling 60.5% stake in SAS. During its November 5 earnings call, the group's CEO Benjamin Smith said the group now hopes to complete the transaction in the first quarter of 2027, subject to European Commission approval. This could eventually support SAS joining the existing Delta–Air France–KLM–Virgin Atlantic transatlantic joint venture.

Beyond Atlanta and Seattle, SAS will increase Copenhagen flights to Tokyo Haneda Airport (HND), Seoul's Incheon International Airport (ICN), and Washington Dulles International Airport (IAD) from six weekly to daily. Chicago and Mumbai will also receive more seats through aircraft upgauging, although SAS has not announced additional frequencies for these two routes.

The A350 upgauges add 34 seats per departure, with capacity rising from 266 to 300 passengers. Mumbai Airport (BOM) is particularly noteworthy, having only launched in October this year, marking SAS's return to India after 17 years. Previously operated five times weekly with the A330, the route will enter its first full summer season with larger aircraft, demonstrating the airline's longer-term ambitions in the subcontinent.

These changes precede SAS's largest-ever aircraft investment, announced in June of this year. The agreement covers up to 40 widebodies, including 18 firm Airbus A330-900 orders, two additional leased A330-900s, ten purchase rights, and up to ten additional Airbus A330-300s. The secondhand A330s will support near-term expansion, while new-generation aircraft will underpin fleet modernization. The investment has a stated list-price value exceeding $10 billion.

The carrier has put pen to paper on the largest investment in its history.

As if that wasn't enough, SAS is also strengthening its European and Scandinavian network to support Copenhagen's development as a major international connecting hub. Flights from Copenhagen to Stockholm Arlanda Airport (ARN) will increase from 15 to 18 daily departures, while Aarhus rises from four to five daily flights. Geneva, Barcelona and Venice will also gain significant additional capacity, improving connections between European destinations and SAS's expanding long-haul network.

Beyond these confirmed increases, SAS also plans more departures from Copenhagen to Nice, Palma de Mallorca, Porto, Bilbao and Catania, although frequencies are not yet confirmed. Stockholm will gain additional flights to Luleå, Kiruna, Milan and Madrid, while Geneva and Keflavik services will operate throughout the summer. Oslo will also see increased frequencies to Manchester, Brussels and Rome.

The expansion also introduces two new seasonal destinations from Copenhagen. Ljubljana Jože Pucnik Airport (LJU) launches June 25, operating twice-weekly until August 6 with a 122-seat Embraer E195. Menorca follows on June 30, with weekly flights until August 4 using a 180-seat Airbus A320neo. Menorca becomes SAS's third Balearic destination, while Ljubljana adds another European capital to its network. Together, the new additions reinforce Copenhagen's role as the airline's principal international hub.

3 Easy Hacks To Make Instant Mashed Potatoes WAY Better

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

You might prefer homemade mashed potatoes, but sometimes you just need a shortcut—and on days like that, grabbing the box is WAY easier. But while the back-of-the-box directions will yield just-fine results, a few easy hacks will give you much better results.

As a recipe developer and professional cook, I don’t often make instant mashed potatoes, but I was excited by the challenge of leveling them up—without adding a bunch of extra ingredients or steps that take the convenience out of this convenience food. So I tried making instant mashed potatoes every way I could think of (including the way the box says!). In the end, I found three tricks that, while super simple, massively improve that final bowl of mashed potatoes.

Here are three ways to instantly upgrade instant mashed potatoes.

Most boxes tell you to rehydrate instant mashed potato flakes in hot water enriched with a little butter and a few tablespoons of milk—but I’m going all in on the milk here. The extra dairy really fills out the flavor of the mashed potato and makes it creamier—especially if you’re using a higher-fat milk, like whole or 2%.

When swapping milk for water, it’s best to increase the liquid amount just slightly. For some reason (totally scientific, I’m sure!), the potato flakes require more milk than water to hydrate properly. I recommend 2 extra tablespoons for every half cup of instant mashed potatoes.

Also, add the extra milk the box typically calls for. If you leave it out, the potatoes will be dry. When I was testing, I found it didn’t really matter when that second amount of milk went into the pan (at the beginning versus just before the potatoes), but some boxes emphasize putting it in cold just before adding the potatoes—so if you want to do that to make you feel safer, be my guest!

All the instant mashed potatoes I made lacked seasoning—until I doubled the salt. Then they were perfect. Now here’s the thing: I was testing with Diamond Crystal kosher salt, which is less salty by volume than table salt or Morton’s kosher. The back of the instant mashed potato box didn’t specify what type of salt they used, so if they tested with a fine salt or Morton’s kosher, maybe ¼ teaspoon was the perfect amount. But in my kitchen with Diamond Crystal, it was woefully underseasoned.

Maybe you’re nervous about oversalting and don’t want to double or even slightly increase the salt right away. But regardless, I’m begging you: Taste those potatoes before you serve them and don’t feel bad if you have to add another hefty pinch.

You might assume that mashed potatoes should be made in a pot—I definitely did. But the fluffiest, creamiest instant potatoes I made in this whole testing process came from a wide, high-sided sauté pan. The reason for this? Absolute science.

When adding the instant potato flakes to the hot liquid, the goal is to distribute them as evenly as possible without working them too much. Yes, the back of the box says to “stir” the potatoes in, but in testing, I quickly discovered that even a few strokes can quickly lead to gummy potatoes.

Here’s where the pan size comes in. Using a wide pan increases the liquid's surface area, so you can sprinkle the potatoes evenly over the top and pat them down into the liquid instead of stirring. From there, let them stand until they’ve absorbed the liquid, then give them a few stirs. Trust me: They’ll be way fluffier because you messed with them less!

Across three days of keynote sessions, conferences, and summits, and hosted in partnership with the State of Qatar's Ministry of Communications and Information Technology (MCIT), delegates explored how embedded intelligence, next-generation infrastructure and national digital strategies are reshaping the Middle East and North Africa's role in the global connectivity ecosystem.

We Read Sofia Franklyn’s ‘Daddy Issues’ Memoir So You Don’t Have to—Here Are the 6 Biggest Bombshells

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

The day has finally come. Sofia “With an F” Frankyln’s tell-all memoir, Daddy Issues, is on shelves now, which means a bunch of details from her infamous fallout with her former Call Her Daddy co-host and best friend, Alex Cooper, are coming to light. Seriously—there’s so much tea in these pages, it even prompted Alix Earle to pick up a copy and apparently read a book for the first time. (Her words, not mine!)

Aside from her reported feud with Alex, Sofia unveiled other shocking details from her personal life throughout the memoir. Here are the six biggest bombshells from Sofia Franklyn’s memoir, Daddy Issues.

Sofia truly wasted no time. In the prologue to her memoir, she revealed Alex’s husband, Matt Kaplan, allegedly called her book agent the day she announced Daddy Issues and threatened to take legal action. It was the first time she had (sort of) been contacted by Alex since their infamous fallout in 2020.

“I remember this feeling of powerlessness. Alex rearranging my life with no acknowledgment. My life being taken, overnight. On the scoreboard of Alex vs. Sofia, Alex won. I cannot fathom her trying to do it again,” Sofia wrote in her memoir. “I feel bulletproof… something born out of a dark place. For the first time I have something Alex wants. Our story. My story.”

Sofia admitted to her tumultuous journey with drugs and alcohol over the years, which eventually led to her multiple arrests. One of her mugshots was even printed on Call Her Daddy merch in June 2020.

She got arrested three separate times while she was in college. In one instance, her fake ID was denied at a bar. She was arrested again when she was caught underage drinking at a DJ set, and a third time when she got “so blackout [drunk]” she “can’t remember what happened.”

Before she married Matt in 2024, Alex was romantically linked to MLB player Noah Syndergaard. They had an on-again, off-again relationship in 2017, and Sofia claimed Alex had been plotting to get back at him when he attempted to reconcile with her over dinner.

“I have a favor to ask,” Alex said, per Sofia’s recollection. “Okay, we’re going to Lure Fishbar and I want you to get a picture.”

“Like, paparazzi-type shit?” Sofia asked, to which Alex allegedly responded, “He’s just such a shit, and I want to have them in case I need to send them to his mom.”

Sofia revealed the photo she took eventually ran in a January 2018 report from Busted Coverage, alleging Alex submitted the tip herself. “Who has access to this photo besides me and her??” she wrote.

According to the “anonymous” tip, Alex was described as “the smoking hot former Boston U soccer player who we’ve met and posted about multiple times.”

When CHD became one of the most popular pods in the biz, the former co-hosts pushed the company to renegotiate their 3-year contract early, asking for $1 million per year in a new contract.

At the time, Barstool maintained the intellectual property of CHD, which was also the main reason Alex and Sofia’s friendship and professional partnership ended. As the girls tried to figure out next steps, they nearly left the company and started their own show called The Fathers.

After Sofia drank heavily at the Barstool holiday party, she claimed she woke up the next morning with little to no recollection of the night before—but she knew she had done something “very wrong.”

When she asked Alex about it, the Unwell founder claimed her behavior was “rude” and “aggressive,” to the point that Dave Portnoy almost “fired” her. Sofia later learned that Alex attempted to use her alleged party fouls as leverage to get a promotion.

“She was like, ‘If I have to put up with her, I need more money,’” Alex’s friend and former CHD employee, Muji, reportedly told Sofia. “So she got the raise.”

In case you need a rundown, here’s what happened: Alex continued CHD under Barstool in 2021, after Sofia wanted to leave and start another podcast. Months after Sofia was left to her own devices, Barstool’s founder, Dave Portnoy, reportedly offered her a solo show.

“She came to me with a plan, and I needed an episode. There’s no clear favorite. You could always have your own show, and we promote the shit out of it like we did with Call Her Daddy. I think it would be huge,” Dave said, according to Sofia. She also claimed Alex had told Dave that she was “never going to sign” the Barstool contract because she was “trying to blow up the deal.”

Sofia never ended up taking his offer and started her own podcast, Sofia With an F, independently in October 2020.

As for Alex, she eventually left Barstool and took CHD with her, signing a $60 million deal with Spotify. She signed another $125 million deal with SiriusXM in 2024.

Dave and Alex have yet to respond to any of Sofia’s allegations in her memoir, if at all.

Resident Evil remakes skipped the first game because Capcom worried it "might not resonate"

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

"We felt that experiencing that was the best way to introduce the origins"

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Capcom's modern Resident Evil remakes are fantastic entry points for one of the most beloved survival horror series of all time, but it's always felt weird that the very first 1996 game still hasn't been given the full-blown third-person RE Engine treatment. Yes, there's a remake from 2002, but it's way more faithful to the original game than the much more recent remakes of Resident Evil 2, 3, and 4, featuring the classic tank controls and static camera from the series' origin. Basically, if you want to start Resident Evil with the first game in the series, you have to deal with quite a few design foundations that'll feel seriously dated if you're unfamiliar. Like ink ribbons. You don't want ink ribbons, trust me.

With how successful the Resident Evil remakes have been, it may seem inevitable that Capcom revisits the very first game. But while rumors suggest there's already a new RE1 early in development, there's been no official announcement or even heavily substantiated reporting to add weight to those rumors. As to why Capcom didn't choose Resident Evil as the obvious starting point for its newer remakes, longtime series producer Jun Takeuchi says the company worried it wouldn't resonate with a modern audience.

"The reason we didn't choose Resident Evil 1 as the first remake is because it's such a stoic game," Takeuchi says in a new book celebrating the series' 30th anniversary (translation via Fandom). "Because it focuses so heavily on the horror derived from its stoicism, we were concerned that it might not resonate with today's game fans."

As is always the case with second-hand translations, it's hard to pin down the exact intent behind translated words like "stoic," but Resident Evil 1 definitely has a distinct vibe and slower pace than newer games in the series. Speaking of which, Takeuchi says the iconic opening sequence from Resident Evil 2, which sets up the mass infection of Raccoon City, is basically where everything kicks off anyway.

"Furthermore, the Raccoon City incident is essentially the starting point of the Resident Evil saga, so we felt that experiencing that was the best way to introduce the origins," he says.

I definitely don't disagree. Resident Evil 2 is arguably the most classic version of Resident Evil you can experience, whether that's the 1998 original or the 2019 remake. That said, as a lifelong fan and noted tank control hater, I would love to see a modern reimagining of the game that started it all.

Where does your favorite land on our ranking of the best Resident Evil games?

PlayStation Cancels In-Development Live-Service Shooter, Fairgames, At Jade Raymond's Former Haven Studio

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

PlayStation has reportedly canceled Fairgames, the long-in-development live-service shooter being made by Haven Studios, which Jade Raymond founded in 2021. Sony acquired Haven in 2022, and in 2025, Raymond left the studio, though it remained dedicated to developing Fairgames without her. It seems the story of Fairgames is now over, according to a new Bloomberg report.

The publication reports that PlayStation will not enforce any job cuts or layoffs as a result of Fairgames' cancellation, which comes as a surprise, and the studio will instead shift to support other first-party teams under the PlayStation umbrella. According to Bloomberg, this cancellation comes after a recent test of the game that allowed "thousands of players" to check it out did not deliver the results PlayStation wanted.

"While the test demonstrated strong operational excellence, live ops readiness and community engagement, player feedback and retention results were mixed and fell short of the goals we had set," PlayStation executives Sean Decker and John Doyle wrote in an email to employees today, according to Bloomberg. "Combined with challenging market conditions in the PVP shooter genre, we have made the difficult decision to cease development on the game."

This doesn't necessarily come as a surprise. PlayStation announced Fairgames back in 2023 and hasn't shown much since, leaving many to assume development has been struggling behind the scenes. Since then, PlayStation has pulled back heavily on its live-service plans, which originally included launching more than 10 live-service games by March of this year. Naughty Dog's Last of Us multiplayer game was canceled, Bungie ended active development on Destiny 2, and live-service Horizon spin-off Hunters Gathering is reportedly being overhauled following poor playtest feedback, amongst other stories. Reading between the lines, many fans were waiting for news like today's cancellation of Fairgames to drop.

Still, in an era where games are canceled and entire teams are laid off (sometimes even after releasing a successful game), it's nice to see Haven employees are not facing layoffs.

When would aliens contact Earth? Astronomers test a new idea

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Astronomers are searching for alien signals at a moving point opposite the Sun, testing a strategy that could help distant civilizations make contact.

Searching for alien signals sounds like a question of which star to aim a telescope at. It’s also a question of when to listen, because a civilization sending a focused radio beam toward Earth might transmit only at selected times.

For the first time, astronomers have specifically searched a moving point in the sky that could offer a shared cue for when to transmit and listen. They found no evidence of extraterrestrial technology.

Sofia Sheikh of the SETI Institute and Eleanor Hort of the University of New Mexico led the search, which used the Allen Telescope Array in Hat Creek, California.

Three nights of pilot observations in the summer of 2023 turned up 1.9 million radio signals, and all of them could be explained by human technology.

Two people who agree to meet in a city but forget to pick a time and place still have a decent chance of finding each other.

Each one picks the landmark the other is most likely to pick. Game theorists call that shared choice a Schelling point.

The team applied the same logic to a distant transmitter. From some directions in the galaxy, an observer would see Earth cross the face of the Sun once a year.

That crossing tells them a planet exists and how long its year is. It also shows the planet orbits at a distance where water can stay liquid.

Halfway through the crossing, Earth lies exactly between the transmitter and the Sun.

Seen from Earth, the transmitter would be in the direction directly opposite the Sun, a spot astronomers call the anti-solar point.

A sender could beam a signal toward Earth at that alignment, while astronomers on Earth listened toward the anti-solar point, without either side arranging contact beforehand.

“The challenge in SETI isn’t simply deciding where to point a telescope,” Sheikh said. “It’s figuring out where and when another civilization might choose to transmit.”

The idea is not new: a 1982 paper proposed it for Jupiter’s crossings, and a 2003 paper argued Earth’s were the better bet.

Yet by 2020, the Breakthrough Listen archive held not a single pointing within half a degree of the anti-solar point.

Two large radio surveys of the 1990s likely spent about 0.0004% of their time there, by accident.

The spot never stays put. As Earth moves around the Sun, the anti-solar point slides along the sky against the background stars, so the telescope has to follow it night after night.

The survey plans repeated observations two to three nights a week over about four years to build coverage along Earth’s orbit.

For the pilot, the team pointed 20 of the array’s 42 dishes at the anti-solar point for about 17 hours over three nights. The scans ran five minutes each and added up to 32 terabytes of data.

They listened between 1.06 and 2.38 gigahertz, a band that includes the radio frequency of hydrogen that early searchers once favored.

Every scan also recorded a second patch of sky nearby.

A signal from a distant point source should show up strongly in the target beam and faintly in the other. Interference from a satellite or a phone tower tends to appear in both.

The two-beam comparison alone removed 1.3 million of the 1.9 million signals. The team then plotted the most promising 2,114 and inspected each one by eye.

None of them looked like a deliberate alien signal.

Most were narrow and barely shifting in frequency, the signature of ground-based transmitters, and the few that drifted lined up with known sources of human interference, including satellite links.

A search that finds nothing still says something, because the old Arecibo planetary radar sent out a tight beam with an effective power of roughly 20 trillion watts.

The pilot was sensitive enough to catch a transmitter that strong from 44 to 124 light-years away.

The exact reach depends on where the signal fell in the beam and how fast its frequency drifted.

The pilot sets the first limit of any kind on alien signals timed to the anti-solar point. The limit applies to a narrow strip, though.

The telescope’s beam spans only about 5% of the width of the zone from which Earth’s crossings are visible. A transmitter slightly above or below that line would have been missed.

The full survey began after the pilot, two to three nights a week at higher frequencies chosen to dodge the worst interference. It has already logged more than 121 nights.

The team plans to report the first year of data in a later paper.

A detection would need a lot to be true: a civilization within reach, one that has noticed Earth, and one that reasons about meeting points the way people do.

The team doesn’t assume any of that; the search is a way to test it. It also assumes the sender has accounted for how long light takes to cross the gap, so the signal arrives when Earth expects it.

Whether anyone is transmitting at the anti-solar point is still an open question, and three nights can’t settle it.

The multiyear survey will test the idea across a much broader stretch of sky.

The full study was published in the journal The Astronomical Journal.

Zendesk moves to pay-per-result AI pricing with its new Outcome AI Platform

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Customers will pay when AI resolves an issue, and a new directory helps AI assistants find official support.

Zendesk is changing how businesses pay for its AI, charging them for problems solved rather than for the number of people using the software. The customer service company announced the change on Tuesday at its AI Summit in San Francisco, as part of a new Outcome AI Platform.

Under the new plans, customers set an ‘Outcome Budget’ and pay for each eligible issue the AI resolves or task it completes. Zendesk checks each result for quality and completeness, so customers can see what counts towards their budget. As a result, businesses can start small and spend more as the AI takes on more work.

Zendesk has backed this model before. Earlier this year, it argued alongside Sierra that AI agents should be paid per result, not per seat, and OpenAI has started trying a similar approach with some customers.

Tapan Patel, a research director at IDC, said the change reflects how business buyers now want to pay for AI. Linking cost to verified results, he added, makes it clearer what customers get for their money.

The platform is also built for a world where people send their own AI assistants to deal with companies. To help with that, Zendesk is launching the Open Service Directory, a public list where brands can register their official support channels. AI assistants can then use it to find verified help, even for brands that do not use Zendesk.

Brands control what they share and when a human must step in. The directory does not access private customer data or take any action itself. Early members include Betterment, ElevenLabs and Telekom Payment.

Meanwhile, a tool called Zendesk Bridge lets businesses connect AI agents and apps of their choice, such as ChatGPT, to Zendesk’s systems. Setting up a connection starts with a name, which is kept for audit logs, and a plain description of what the outside agent should and should not do.

Administrators then choose exactly what each agent may do, such as reading, creating, answering, updating or merging tickets. An assistant called Admin Copilot suggests which tools to switch on, based on the description.

Every action then passes through Bridge, which checks permissions and company rules and asks for human approval where needed. An observability page shows how many requests came from AI agents and how many from people, which ones were blocked and why. It also includes a kill switch. Bridge is in early access, and the directory is launching soon.

“People don’t think about which channels, systems, or departments they need to navigate to interact with a business,” said Zendesk chief executive Tom Eggemeier.

Other new tools, now generally available, help teams build and improve their AI agents. AI Agent Launchpad studies past customer conversations and suggests where AI could take over. In Zendesk’s demo for a scooter rental firm, it proposes a step-by-step process for rides that will not end, using a connected billing system such as Stripe.

Similarly, Agentic Analytics lets managers ask questions about their data in plain language, such as how many tickets are still open. They can then see whether changes are cutting repeat contacts.

The launch reflects changing habits, according to a Zendesk survey. It found that 52% of consumers are comfortable relying on personal AI assistants for everyday tasks. Even so, 84% said human help should always be available.

The announcement follows Zendesk’s purchase of Forethought, its biggest deal in two decades, as it builds out its AI agents.

Anthony Mackie, Ariana DeBose, and John Leguizamo reveal why they joined Andy Weir's sci-fi audio drama 'Exoplanet' (interview)

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

'It's coming from brilliant minds who build worlds so impeccably. And it's about real stuff we’re dealing with today.'

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

In any respectable audiobook project, finding the names of Andy Weir ("The Martian," "Project Hail Mary") and Benjamin Percy ("The Ninth Metal," "Wolverine") attached would elicit intense fan interest, but add the superstar vocal talents of Anthony Mackie ("Avengers: Infinity War," "Avengers: Doomsday"), Ariana DeBose ("West Side Story"), and John Leguizamo ("Ice Age"), and it catapults this sonic odyssey into the stratosphere.

Such is the case with "Exoplanet," Audible Original’s new 7-episode science fiction audio drama executive produced by Weir and penned by Percy. Its planet-hopping space disaster narrative sees the crew of the starship Wild Blue Yonder hit a quantum anomaly, forcing their ship to crash-land onto a deadly exoplanet. All is not as it seems, though, as each crew member arrives in a different era on the same world.

We hooked up with Mackie, DeBose, and Lequizamo to learn more about this renaissance of audio dramas and what attracted them to board Weir's sci-fi thriller.

One crew. One planet. Three timelines. No one survives alone. A sci-fi thriller executive produced by Andy Weir (#1 bestselling author of Project Hail Mary, The Martian), with an all-star cast led by Anthony Mackie, Ariana DeBose and John Leguizamo.

Exoplanet is free for Audible members, but also available for purchase separately.

Mackie voices Chief Engineer Jeff Shaw. His attraction to the imaginative genre began at an early age thanks to some cool family friends while growing up in the Big Easy.

"My brother went to college at Morehouse," he tells SPACE. "I was like 8, and he was 18. He’d always bring friends home, because New Orleans is a party city, so they'd come over and get drunk. But one of his friends gave me a book by Walter Mosley and one by Octavia Butler, and I got hooked into sci-fi and the future."

"From 'Back to the Future' to 'Aliens,' the future was such an idea of what was going to be out there. Now we’re living it," reveals Mackie. "It’s very important to me as a human being to have that construct as a part of my career. The only thing more arrogant than wanting to be president is standing up and saying, 'Yeah, I can go to space.' That sounds crazy to me."

Leguizamo's New York City childhood was influenced by absorbing classic radio dramas of the past, which primed him for this special Audible project.

“It was Anthony and Ariana being in it that really pumped me up, and of course Andy Weir," he recalls regarding his portrayal of Science Officer Logan Bowers. "Then when I got the theme, that it was something relevant today and makes you think about computers and technology taking over and making us obsolete. It just hit me in that right zeitgeist, at that right little point."

"As a poor kid, there was no YouTube or cell phones, and we didn’t have computers," reminisces Leguizamo. "I used to go to the Broadcast Museum on 53rd Street and listen to old radio plays like The Shadow and these incredible detective radio plays. It's a different art form. With no visuals, your voice has to create the world. I thought that was an incredible challenge."

DeBose portrays the Wild Blue Yonder's confident First Officer Becca Bang, who’s stranded in a temporal mystery that might engulf her at any moment.

"I've yet to do anything like this, which is always exciting," she adds. "I was a little afraid of it, which told me I should do it. These gents are so talented. It's great company. It’s coming from brilliant minds who build worlds so impeccably. And it's about real stuff we’re dealing with today. I liked the idea that we could tell this story and make people listen and create a space for them to imagine themselves in it and see how they problem-solve to make the world a bit better."

Recording your character's lines is far more difficult than just stepping up to the microphone to read a script, and our ace "Exoplanet" trio delivered in every session.

"I just go in and throw myself at something," DeBose recalls. "I don’t really have a method. But I can tell you this: it is a full-body workout. I use everything at my disposal, whether it’s a chair or a pillow or running in place. I just do what the character does and end up sweating. And after three days, I have to go sit in silence. It's great. I love the idea of bringing back the radio play. The art of listening is such a thing. I don’t think people listen enough. Even for myself."

Likewise, Leguizamo embraced his booth sessions with exuberance and authenticity.

"You have to cancel the rest of your day, especially if you've got to talk, because your vocal chords are fried," he admits. "And if they're not fried you're not doing your job. You really have to do the physicality. If you run you have to run like a maniac and be out of breath. If I'm getting hit I start punching myself. Then there’s a sexiness to doing these things. Your voice has to have a little mystery, a little naughtiness. It’s an interesting intersection where you pull it back a little and invite the audience in.”

For Mackie, who’s used to athleticism needed to play MCU superheroes like The Falcon and Captain America, recording dialogue offered a different type of opportunity.

“I look for reasons not to talk to people, so I love doing these," he jokes. "Once I’m out of the studio, I’m not talking to anybody for two days. At a certain point for me, I find mentally I get tired of hearing myself talk. I just have to come home at cut off. I’ve said enough words for the next month.”

"Exoplanet" is available now exclusively from Audible.

One crew. One planet. Three timelines. No one survives alone. A sci-fi thriller executive produced by Andy Weir (#1 bestselling author of Project Hail Mary, The Martian), with an all-star cast led by Anthony Mackie, Ariana DeBose and John Leguizamo.

Exoplanet is free for Audible members, but also available for purchase separately.

His work has appeared at SYFY Wire, Inverse, Collider, Bleeding Cool and elsewhere. Jeff lives in beautiful Bend, Oregon amid the ponderosa pines, classic muscle cars, a crypt of collector horror comics, and two loyal English Setters.

Strategy Adds 1,036 BTC While Repurchasing $188M of STRC Preferred Shares

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

Strategy’s newest capital-allocation update contains two transactions that point in different directions. In a November 9 filing announcement, the company said it acquired 1,036 additional Bitcoin and bought back approximately $188 million of STRC preferred shares. The first adds to exposure to BTC; the second reduces outstanding securities in its wider financing structure.

TL;DR: Strategy has disclosed a 1,036-BTC purchase alongside $188 million of STRC repurchases. The combination shows that the company’s Bitcoin strategy involves ongoing capital management, not simply accumulation. These are separate transactions with different effects on liquidity, risk and future obligations.

Strategy is closely associated with long-term Bitcoin accumulation, and for good reason: its treasury is far larger than that of most public companies. Yet the recurring filings increasingly include details about preferred securities, reserves and capital raising that are central to understanding how that position is financed.

A Bitcoin purchase turns available funds into an asset whose market value can change around the clock. Repurchasing preferred shares does something else. It reduces the quantity of a security issued to outside investors and may alter future distribution obligations, depending on that security’s terms. One action raises direct BTC exposure; the other changes the claims that investors have on Strategy’s capital structure.

That distinction was visible in an earlier update covered by NewsBTC, when Strategy bought 811 BTC and repurchased $208.2 million of STRC. The new announcement continues that paired approach, rather than revealing a sudden switch away from its central investment thesis.

Preferred securities can offer investors contractual distributions or other rights that differ from ordinary equity. Their terms, market prices and the company’s reserve position matter for both existing holders and common shareholders. Buying them back may be attractive when they trade at prices the issuer considers advantageous, but it also uses liquidity that could otherwise remain in reserve or fund more purchases.

There is a useful comparison with other Bitcoin treasury companies, which have their own funding methods and varying tolerance for leverage. The phrase ‘corporate Bitcoin buyer’ hides important differences in debt maturities, preferred distributions, equity issuance and the sources of cash used to acquire coins.

Strategy’s public metrics indicate Bitcoin holdings around 850,364 BTC following the update. Readers should use the company’s dated disclosure when comparing holdings and purchase prices rather than combining a fresh transaction with an older snapshot. Market-value figures can change between the filing and the moment a reader opens the article.

Another consideration is the difference between a repurchase authorization and completed repurchases. Here the company is describing actual STRC repurchase activity, which should be counted separately from any standing authority to buy securities later. The price paid and reduction in outstanding shares determine its economic impact; simply repeating the dollar amount cannot answer those questions.

None of this proves that the company’s securities or Bitcoin will rise. A more useful test is whether Strategy can keep servicing its obligations while making purchases in a period of changing interest rates and volatile digital-asset prices. The November 9 announcement confirms it is still doing both jobs—adding coins and adjusting the financing architecture that supports the strategy.

The Cat In The Hat Is Another Box Office Flop Warner Bros. Can't Afford

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Another weekend, another bad result for Warner Bros. "The Cat in the Hat" looked like it could help get Warner Bros. back on track at the box office. Instead? It became the latest in a string of flops that has defined the studio's year. Family-friendly movies have been very safe bets in recent years, and on paper, Dr. Seuss is very good intellectual property to exploit for a PG animated movie. But this didn't pan out as anyone had hoped.

Director Erica Rivinoja's "The Cat in the Hat" opened to a very disappointing $20 million domestically. It also pulled in just $10 million internationally, giving it a soft $30 million global start. That was only good enough for the number two spot, since "Godzilla Minus Zero" ruled the box office with a $30.5 million opening. Normally, it would be much safer to bet on the PG family movie over the R-rated Japanese monster movie. But this wasn't a normal situation, and instead, the animated movie was left fighting for scraps, relatively speaking.

The new movie sees the title character (voiced by "SNL" alum Bill Hader) take on his toughest assignment yet for the I.I.I.I. (Institute for the Institution of Imagination and Inspiration, LLC): to cheer up Gabby and Sebastian, a pair of siblings struggling with their move to a new town. It carries a reported budget in the $90 million range.

This major disappointment comes mere weeks after the Warner Bros. Discovery and Paramount merger was made official, with the mega-corporation Skydance now ruling over both studios. That corporation is lousy with debt and it needs Warner Bros. to deliver. Unfortunately for CEO David Ellison and his new film chiefs, they're walking into a messy situation. They've got a ship to right.

The storied movie studio had a stellar year in 2025, with massive hits like "Sinners" and "F1" to its name. Warner Bros. set a huge record that year with six movies in a row opening to at least $40 million, but 2026 has not been nearly as kind.

Skydance inherited a string of massive flops from the Warner Bros. deal, with its 2027 slate also looking a bit uneven. Things could get worse before they get better, as evidenced by the performance of "The Cat in the Hat." Granted, the year should end on a high note as "Dune: Part 3" is just around the corner, but even that can't make up for everything that preceded it.

A very rough rule of thumb for theatrical success has always been a movie making 2.5 times its budget, given that theaters keep around half of all money from ticket sales. So just how bad has the year been for Warner Bros? The studio's bombs/disappointments include "The Bride!" ($90 million budget / $24 million worldwide haul), "They Will Kill You" ($10 million budget / $19 million worldwide), "Mortal Kombat II" ($80 million budget / $129 million worldwide), "Supergirl" ($170 million budget / $126 million worldwide), "The End of Oak Street" ($90 million budget / $121 million worldwide), "Practical Magic 2" ($75 million budget / $106 million worldwide), and "Digger" ($180 million budget / $50 million worldwide).

Yikes. Tom Cruise's "Digger" alone will lose as much as $250 million. Hits like "Wuthering Heights" and "Clayface," encouraging though they may be, can't come close to making up for losses that pile up for would-be blockbusters like "Supergirl." Unfortunately, this Dr. Seuss adaptation now joins this ever-growing list of crushing disappointments. Skydance and the new team has their work cut out for them.

Why Ari Aster and Lars Knudsen’s Square Peg Built a Therapy Circle for Hollywood’s Next Generation in Texas

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Talent labs and mentorship programs are plentiful for aspiring filmmakers — but where do you turn when your career has already started, after your first movie or on the precipice of one, and especially when filmmaking can be such a lonely enterprise?

Enter Square Peg Social, the Austin, Texas-based filmmaking summit launched by Lars and Amy Knudsen and Ari Aster that offers four days of singular community-building: brutally honest advice, commiseration, and idea exchange shared among 35 participants, with the program now in its second year.

A weekend of panels, discussion, and camaraderie designed for filmmakers at the early stages of their career, this year’s talent-incubating event was even crashed by the likes of Zach Cregger and Richard Linklater — both of whom were down to get frank about their filmmaking highs and lows. (In fall 2025, IndieWire did a deep dive into Square Peg Social’s first year, just to show how much the event has already grown.) Related Stories ‘Ink’ Star Jack O’Connell on His ‘New Era’ as a Leading Man: ‘Thank F**k for Danny Boyle’ How Casting the New ‘Harry Potter’ Helped ‘Club Kid’ Find Its Adorable 10-Year-Old Star

But the program’s stable of 2026 mentors and panelists — i.e., filmmakers keen not only to open up to new filmmakers about their own struggles, but also to scout new talent — also counted “Primetime” and “You Can See Everything” director Lance Oppenheim, “Sing Sing” filmmaker Greg Kwedar, “The First Omen” director Arkasha Stevenson, and “Mother Mary” director David Lowery among them.

The closed-door, off-the-record, noncompetitive summit opened to applicants in May, later unfolding from September 17-21. Partners including the Austin Film Society, Belvedere, and Airbnb offset the cost, besides a modest application fee, for participants to hole up for four days and nights at Soho House that don’t promise job opportunities — but instead, the kind of lasting industry connections that can take years (and many starts and stops) to build. Also, no job opportunities isn’t entirely true. Take last year’s Square Peg Social mentee Yona Strauss, who’s now working for Knudsen and Aster’s Square Peg.

Just a sampling of this year’s participating producers and directors: producer Annie Huang, who worked on franchise IP like “Barbie” and “Wicked” at major studios; Jack Auen, co-founder of the filmmaking collective Cosmic Salon, which this year directed the New York critics’ favorite “Chronovisor”; Emmy-winning director of the Netflix short “The Turnaround,” Kyle Thrash; SXSW 2026 Neon Auteur Award winner Oliver Woolf; and many more who have their foot in the door, but are ready to cross the threshold.

IndieWire spoke to Lars Knudsen and Aster (who also co-founded the production company Square Peg, which this year backed “The Drama” and “Primetime”), plus Stevenson, Oppenheim, and Kwedar, along with three participants, to replay the on-the-ground experience in Austin. Late nights, early mornings, and vulnerabilities abounded across a long (but in some ways too short) weekend of intimate panels, small group sessions, and candid remarks from established filmmakers about the times they were trying to hold their own projects together with Scotch tape and paper clips. Think of it like a mini summer camp devoted to obsessive ambition.

“When you’ve been doing it for this long, sometimes it feels like you’re just a little bit going through the motions. You’re going from movie to movie to movie, and you sometimes forget why we do this thing that we do. We’re in this world working with these artists where they don’t have a plan B. Having now worked with 30, 40 filmmakers, I realized that when my wife brought up the idea of doing something because she has her own aversions to the industry, and that there isn’t really — it sounds cliché — but there just isn’t a lot of kindness or compassion or openness in our industry.

“There’s a lot of fear, there’s a lot of rejection. We wanted to create a space for filmmakers where there’s radical transparency and where it was truly, truly focused on a selfless thing: helping young filmmakers navigate the industry at a time in their career where things can either take off or not, and really demystifying the process of making movies, especially during the stage where they’re often in the back seat, which is financing or casting. Because for all these filmmakers that I work with and all the filmmakers that we’ve invited there, even the producers, it’s the first thing to think about when they wake up and the last thing to think about when they go to bed. Nothing is more important. I wanted to create an event where that is treated with the utmost importance, almost like it’s a life-or-death thing because it is all they want to do.”

“When Lars and Amy first broached the idea with me, I realized that it was something that I wish I had when I got out of school [at AFI], because I really did have to navigate that on my own. I was making a lot of shorts with whatever money I could raise and writing screenplays and trying to get one screenplay going. When that failed, writing something else and really looking for any sort of mentorship or guidance, It’s a very demoralizing process, and it was very, very hard for me to find traction, and it hasn’t gotten easier. It’s as bad as it’s ever been. There’s just so much uncertainty right now in the industry, not just for filmmakers. Even the lives of these studios are suddenly in danger and in question.

“There are a lot of filmmakers that Lars and I have been championing and films that we’ve been trying to get financing for that have proven almost impossible… It felt like a good thing to create a space where filmmakers can come together and commiserate and share experiences and connect. More important than even the connections that are made between the mentors and the mentees here are actually the relationships made among the people who are attending. A lot of them end up working together because we have producers and directors coming in. Last year proved really successful, and I would just say it was refreshing for us, too, that it galvanized us to keep doing this. Because last year it was almost an experiment: Will this work? How will we feel having done this? … Everybody walked away feeling good and feeling like it was a really useful and positive experience.”

“I’ve never really had the opportunity to just hang out with so many great filmmakers, not only just working filmmakers whose work I’ve loved, like David Lowery, who I’ve idolized for so long, but there’s so many filmmakers that were actually in the program as participants whose work I also have really admired. So having a space where we could all just kind of talk and vent and really nerd out on stuff that you never really get the opportunity to do.

“Because this was so filmmaker-driven and filmmaker-led, it encouraged the spirit of vulnerability [where] everyone really went there. I felt like maybe I should go back to therapy or something, but I felt like this was a great opportunity to therapize and also dump all of my feelings into a forum where you could just be open and honest about the process of making films… Most people had made features before, and were in the process of making features. It felt much more like we were at a film festival and everyone was able to connect with each other in a much deeper way, more than anything I’ve been to before.

“The first night I was there, I stayed up till like three in the morning, and I went on one of those electric scooters, the limes with Lars and Christian [Vasquez], my producer, and a producer from the event, too. We went to Whataburger and just kept talking. I think that was sort of the vibe. I don’t know. I guess everyone was encouraged to hang out for as long as they wanted to and to kick back and relax. There was another night we closed down the Soho House lounge bar, and Amy was like, ‘Let’s keep hanging out.’ So we were outside the bar, and everybody pulled together all of the drinks in their mini bar, and we created an ad hoc bar outside of the Soho House, and we kept drinking and hanging.”

“I don’t think you can really do film if you have a plan B because there’s always an escape hatch, and film requires every single atom of your body, mind and soul. It naturally becomes a very therapeutic place. I think because we’re all in this constant state of obsession, that can get really tiring, and it can get really lonely. … [The industry] attracts very sensitive souls. [With Square Peg Social] I didn’t even realize how badly I needed it until the first year. I’d known Amy and Lars before that, and Amy said that she wanted to start a Social. … I thought it would be much smaller, smaller. It ended up being this great big thing where, yeah, it does feel like a therapy circle, really.

“Everyone was really struck by the safety of the event. There was this unspoken agreement that everybody would be checking your egos at the door, and that this would be a very safe place to share your personal vulnerabilities, which is exceptionally rare in film because resources are so limited, and it’s just naturally a very competitive industry. Letting yourself be very, very vulnerable in front of your peers is something you were, in a sense, trained not to do but sometimes desperately need. At Square Peg, also, the line between mentee and mentor blurs quite a bit, which is why I think it’s so beneficial for everybody who goes there.

“It kind of reminds me of summer camp a little bit because you’re up until two or three in the morning talking to people, and then you’re up at eight again.”

“I always think of this term, like, whenever you take an elevator up, if you get to the top floor, send it back down for someone else to get on. [Square Peg] is one of the busiest production companies working today. To dedicate this weekend for emerging artists to have a forum where they can come and feel completely comfortable being very vulnerable with their ambitions, but also the things that they’re afraid of, and to hear that echoed from industry mentors who are being just as vulnerable about their ambitions and the things that they struggle with and the heartbreaks they’ve had, it’s an amazing table that’s set.

“I had ulterior motives in the, in the sense that Clint [Bentley] and I have started this new company, Ethos, and we have a deal at Netflix to finance films from emerging artists, and so I was also there … to look for talent. We’ll be following up with a lot of these artists to just hear more about the films they want to make. One thing that I appreciated about it was that it wasn’t like a pitch fest. You’re not there to pitch your project. You’re there to soak it in and learn.

“It had this healthy blend of these panels, and not the normal kind of panel. One was a panel of filmmakers led off by Ari and Lars, telling a very, very brutally honest story of how hard one of their films was to make. That kind of created a pathway for how the rest of the weekend would unfold.”

“I think the whole reason anyone works in film is that we were once kids making videos in the woods with our friends, making with a shitty camera. And there’s nothing else in this world, and I think I probably speak for everyone, where I can’t imagine film is the only thing that’s our main passion and hobby and something we can also make a career out of. So there was never a plan B at all. Whenever someone asked me, ‘What do you think you’d do if you couldn’t do anything film-adjacent?’ I have literally no idea, maybe a teacher or something. That’s super nice that Lars and the rest of Square Peg was saying that there is no plan B. There’s only: If it takes longer to get to Plan A, then it takes longer to get to Plan A.

“I remember on the very first day before we even properly began, Lars did a little speech with Ari, and Lars was like, ‘We’re going to be 100 percent transparent with you guys.’ And I remember thinking, ‘Oh, that’s cool, but I wonder how transparent.’ And by the end of it, I was like, ‘Fuck me, that was transparent.’ There really was nothing off the table.”

“It was definitely something that I’ve never experienced before in this industry, to experience that level of candor and to hear these traumatic experiences that they have gone through in not a sensationalistic way, but almost in a very personal and almost relatable way, despite me having never experienced that level of millions of dollars of movie budget making — but on a personal level of how the heartbreaks had felt for them individually and the hardships of filmmaking. Coming into this program, there are so many people within our cohort who are incredibly talented, who have accomplished so many things. And coming into it, oftentimes you’ll have a fear of: ‘OK, how do I compare in relation to their accomplishments?’ But in hearing Lars and Ari talk about their own hardships, I think that’s what influenced me to really share some of mine coming up in the industry and producing independently.

“Some of the traumatic experiences that I have also faced are ones that I feel like I have to not talk about or hide from people, for fear of them looking like mistakes or of me looking like I’m not good enough to be doing this. And we were able to share that, like Ollie [Woolf] and I, in our own little producer’s therapy session with the mentors, with Lars. That was really healing for me.”

“[Ari, Lars, and the mentors are] on, not a different level, but they’re all going through similar issues and this and that, whether it’s a low budget or a higher budget type of ordeal. Everyone’s going through the same thing in their own way, in a sense. Just to know the people that I’ve been looking up to for a very long time, the reason in large part why I got into making films, to know that they’re going through similar things and just keep going, and it’s fine. No matter what level we’re going to be at, there’s still something to navigate. So I thought it was very cool to see, over the course of five days, to hear these individual experiences and stuff and how you could relate it back to what we’re going through right now and where we are.

“It was information overload in the best way possible. At a certain point, I hope I’m retaining everything that was said over those five days because it was nonstop, all useful information. I wasn’t even taking notes at a certain point because I’m like, ‘I can’t write all this stuff down. I’m just going to try and soak it all in.'”

Walmart’s 2026 Black Friday and Cyber Monday Sales: Dates, Early Deals, and What to Expect

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If you're thinking of picking up any big-ticket items soon—whether for yourself or as a gift for someone on your list— I would advise you to wait until you see what retailers have in store for the holiday sales season, which will kick off in earnest in mid-November. Walmart is the first major retailer to announce its Black Friday and Cyber Monday plan for 2026, and they actually involve three major shopping events between now and the December holidays.

As it did in 2025, Walmart will hold three sales between Nov. 12 and Dec. 2, as outlined in a recent press release. The sales will be both online and in-store, and will target a mix of Walmart+ members and general shoppers.

The events will take place on three different dates:

Walmart's Black Friday Deals Event 1: Nov. 12–15

Available online and in stores starting at 6 a.m. local time

Walmart+ members get six hours of early access beginning online at 12:01 a.m. ET on November 12

Walmart's Black Friday Deals Event 2: Nov. 23–29

Online only: Nov. 23 starting at 12:01 a.m. ET

Online and in stores: Nov. 27 starting at 6 a.m. local time

Walmart+ members get six hours of early access online beginning Nov. 23 at 12:01 a.m. ET

Walmart's Cyber Monday Deals Event: Nov. 30-Dec. 2

Walmart's physical stores will open at 6:00 a.m. local time on November 27 for their Black Friday events. Of course, they will start online earlier, so make sure to check their website as well.

Walmart says that you can expect discounts on electronics, toys, fashion, beauty, home goods, gifts, and everyday essentials. Although the company doesn't provide much in the way of specifics in its press release, last year's sales included discounts on TCL TVs, Dyson vacuums, Dreo appliances, and toys from LEGO.

Walmart already has plenty of good deals out early. You don't need to wait for the official sale to start to find some products going for their lowest prices ever. Here are some of the best ones I've found so far:

No. But if you are a Walmart+ member, you'll get early access to the sale. You can sign up for a free 30-day Walmart+ subscription and cancel it afterwards or get the annual plan for $98 ($8.17/month).

Deals will be both online and in the physical stores. There might be deals online that you won't see in-stores and vice versa.

The holiday season will see major sales from the same list of retailers that compete for your dollars throughout the year—namely, Best Buy, Target, and Amazon. Each will have its own version of Black Friday and Cyber Monday sales. So far, only Walmart has revealed its plans.

In short, yes. Though they aren't quite as impactful as they once were, Black Friday and Cyber Monday sales offer discounts that can be rare throughout the rest of the year. Whether it's a major purchase you've been saving up for or you’re shopping for gifts, it’s a good time to look for discounts, especially on items like appliances and electronics. Last year's sales included categories such as tech, home improvement, and fitness.

Black Friday used to be when major retailers offered their best deals on expensive tech and appliances, while Cyber Monday was for cheaper tech and gave smaller businesses a chance to compete online. Now, however, the distinction is almost meaningless: Every major retailer offers sales on both days, which means the smart move is to know what you want, use price trackers to monitor the deals, and refer to our Black Friday coverage, where we do all the searching, curating, and price tracking for you.

Report: Liverpool enter transfer battle with Barcelona for €25m star

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Liverpool have been linked with a whole host of players ahead of the winter transfer window.

Many areas among their ranks need upgrading and, even though fans knew this during the summer, Liverpool still only signed four new players.

And, one of them (Jeremy Jacquet) was already confirmed all the way back in January.

Now that we're a few months into the season, it's clear that some cracks are starting to show among the Reds' ranks.

So, with the January transfer window now edging closer, the rumour mill is likely to go into overdrive.

Liverpool and Barcelona have both set their eyes on Juventus' Andrea Cambiaso

Liverpool's full-backs have been a major problem for them this term.

Left-back has been the main issue but, there have been times when some dodgy situations have occurred at right-back.

Indeed, Ronald Araujo has performed much better than Kostas Tsimikas or Milos Kerkez have all season but, it still feels like an upgrade could be beneficial, or, at least someone with a bit of a higher ceiling than the Uruguayan needs to be brought in.

Because, while the defender is good, he's going to be 28 years old by June 2027 which is usually when the performance levels of players start to drop.

So, that's why this latest news from TuttoJuve should pique the interest of fans.

"Barcelona aren't the only team to have included Cambiaso on their list of targets," they wrote.

"Liverpool and Manchester United have also reportedly expressed interest in the Italian full-back, who is particularly appreciated for his ability to cover multiple roles on the wings.

"In particular, Manchester United are reportedly evaluating reinforcements for the left side of defence, while Liverpool are reportedly continuing to monitor various profiles to broaden their options.

"Cambiaso's versatility, which allows him to play on both flanks and in different playing systems, represents a particularly attractive characteristic for major European clubs.

"But one question remains: would €20-25 million be enough to convince Juventus to give up on a player with these characteristics?"

Andrea Cambiaso's versatility is a major selling point for the Italian

Versatility has become a key part of football in recent years.

Clubs now need players to be able to play in multiple positions if needed.

Because, with the sheer number of injuries that they suffer, having just one back-up option for each position isn't really enough anymore.

Cambiaso has been a regular feature for Juventus over the last few years and his ability to play on either side of the pitch is massively beneficial to any club that signs him.

The Italy international is also only 26 years old so, while he already has a lot of experience under his belt, there is still room for him to improve.

Right now, it's unclear what the future holds for Cambiaso but, if Liverpool are serious about reigniting their interest in him, they're going to have to be quick, especially with Barcelona lurking.
