How the Congressional Review Act lets Congress overturn agency rules

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The Congressional Review Act lets Congress cancel federal agency rules, and these 10 explainers cover how it works, from major rules and Senate fast-track votes to the lookback period

Congress writes the laws, but federal agencies write many of the detailed rules that carry the force of law. Lawmakers routinely hand agencies the authority to fill in details on workplace safety, financial markets, energy use and much more. That delegation raises a basic question about accountability. Once an agency finalizes a rule, how can Congress take back a specific decision it never voted on?

The Congressional Review Act is Congress's statutory answer. Passed in 1996 and signed by President Bill Clinton, the law gives Congress a set procedure to overturn individual rules issued by executive-branch agencies. It does not require lawmakers to rewrite the underlying statute. Congress instead passes a short measure called a joint resolution of disapproval, aimed at a single rule.

Both chambers must pass the same resolution, which then goes to the president. That final step shapes much of how the law is used. The president can veto the resolution, and overriding a veto takes two-thirds of each chamber. Successful uses have mostly come when the president and congressional majorities share a party and the targeted rule came from a predecessor.

Timing also shapes the outcome. The law sets windows for introducing resolutions and for using a special Senate procedure that sidesteps the filibuster. Through the lookback period, rules submitted late in a congressional session can become targets for the next Congress. That leaves rules finalized late in an administration exposed when power changes hands.

A successful disapproval has lasting effects. The canceled rule is treated as though it never took effect. The agency also faces limits on issuing a similar rule later, under statutory language that has never been precisely defined.

The law produced no successful disapproval until 2001. That first success stood alone for years. That changed in 2017, when a Republican Congress and President Donald Trump used it to overturn many Obama-era rules.

The 10 slides that follow explain how Congress cancels federal rules under this law. They cover what counts as a rule, how agencies report to Congress, the special treatment of major rules and the procedures in each chamber. They also explain the president's role and what happens after a disapproval. Each slide stands on its own, so readers can start with whichever part of the process they need.

Congress passed the Congressional Review Act in 1996, and President Bill Clinton signed it into law on March 29 of that year. The law gives Congress a formal way to overturn rules that executive-branch agencies issue under powers Congress handed to them.

The act did not arrive as a standalone bill. Lawmakers enacted it as one part of a larger package, the Contract with America Advancement Act. Clinton's signature on that broader measure put the review process into federal law.

The statute is codified at 5 U.S.C. sections 801 to 808. Those eight sections hold the full framework, from how agencies report new rules to what happens after Congress acts on them. That placement gives the process a fixed legal address. Lawmakers and agencies work from the same set of provisions each time a rule comes up for review.

The law responds to a problem that starts with delegation. Congress often writes statutes in broad terms. It then assigns the detailed work of carrying them out to federal agencies.

Those agencies write rules that fill in the specifics. Congress does not vote on each of those rules before an agency issues it. The authority to write them comes from statutes Congress has already passed.

The 1996 law added a check rather than a rewrite of that system. Agencies kept the rulemaking authority Congress had already granted them. What changed was that Congress gained a set procedure for answering a finished rule.

The law's scope follows from that arrangement. It applies to rules from executive-branch agencies acting under authority Congress delegated. The branch that granted the power keeps a statutory path to cancel a particular use of it.

The Congressional Review Act covers a wide range of agency actions. Its definition of "rule" comes from the Administrative Procedure Act, the law that sets out how federal agencies make regulations.

That borrowed definition is broad. A document does not need to carry the label "regulation" to fall within it. What matters is whether the document meets the statutory terms.

The review law then carves out three categories. The first is rules of particular applicability. These are actions aimed at a specific party or circumstance rather than at the public generally.

The second exclusion covers rules about internal agency management. Such rules stay outside the law only if they do not substantially affect parties outside the agency. A directive that reaches no one beyond the agency's own operations would meet that condition.

The third exclusion covers rules of agency organization, procedure or practice. A similar condition attaches. These rules escape review only if they do not substantially affect the rights or obligations of outside parties.

The qualifier in the last two exclusions limits their reach. A procedural or management rule that changes what outside parties must do, or what they are entitled to, does not fit either exemption. Such a rule remains subject to review.

Guidance documents have been a point of dispute. Agencies issue guidance to explain how they read a statute or how they intend to apply it. Whether a given guidance document qualifies as a rule under the review law has been contested.

The Government Accountability Office has issued opinions on such questions. Those opinions address whether a particular agency document meets the statute's definition of a rule. Each opinion applies the statutory definition and its three exclusions to one specific document.

The definition sets the threshold for everything else in the statute. An agency action that falls outside it cannot be overturned through a joint resolution of disapproval under this law.

Federal agencies must send each new rule, along with a report on it, to both chambers of Congress and to the Comptroller General before the rule can take effect. That delivery opens Congress's chance to review the rule under the Congressional Review Act.

The Comptroller General heads the Government Accountability Office, or GAO. The submission therefore goes to three recipients: the House, the Senate and the head of the GAO. Each receives the rule itself and the agency's report on it.

The duty is not limited to the costliest regulations. It applies to every action that meets the law's definition of a rule, whether or not the rule is classified as major. A routine regulation and a sweeping one face the same delivery requirement.

Finalizing a regulation is not the agency's last step. A rule cannot take effect until the agency has completed this submission. The requirement ties a new rule's legal effect to Congress being told about it.

Congress treats receipt of the rule as the start of its review clock. The deadline for introducing a disapproval resolution counts forward from that date. The window for the Senate's expedited procedure also runs from receipt, together with the rule's publication.

The date of receipt therefore anchors the whole review. Every later procedural deadline depends on it. A lawmaker weighing a challenge first needs to know when Congress received the rule.

Rules that agencies never formally submitted have been a recurring issue under the law. The problem follows from how the review clock works. If Congress never formally receives a rule, the event that starts the review period has not happened. The delivery step is what links an agency's rulemaking to Congress's power to overturn it.

A major rule under the Congressional Review Act generally cannot take effect until 60 days after it is submitted to Congress or published in the Federal Register, whichever comes later. The waiting period applies only to rules that meet the law's definition of major.

An agency does not apply the label to itself. The Office of Information and Regulatory Affairs makes the determination. Its finding rests on what a rule is likely to do once it is in force.

The first trigger is a dollar threshold. A rule is major if it is likely to have an annual effect on the economy of $100 million or more.

A second trigger looks at prices. A rule also qualifies as major if it is likely to cause a major increase in costs or prices.

The third trigger covers wider economic harm. A rule qualifies if it is likely to have significant adverse effects on competition, employment, investment, productivity or innovation. Likely significant adverse effects on U.S. competitiveness also count.

Each trigger stands on its own. A rule with an expected annual effect below $100 million can still be major if it is likely to significantly harm employment or competition.

The 60-day clock runs from the later of two dates. One is the day the agency submits the rule to Congress. The other is the day the rule is published in the Federal Register. If an agency publishes a rule and submits it to Congress afterward, the countdown starts on the submission date.

The delay gives lawmakers time to examine a high-impact rule before it takes effect. Rules that fall short of the major tests are not held by this 60-day pause.

The Government Accountability Office works on a tighter deadline. It must send Congress a report on each major rule within 15 days. That deadline falls inside the 60-day waiting period.

A joint resolution of disapproval is the one tool the Congressional Review Act gives lawmakers to cancel a federal agency rule. The statute fixes its wording: Congress disapproves a named rule, and that rule shall have no force or effect.

The prescribed text is a single sentence with blanks. It states that Congress disapproves the rule submitted by a named agency relating to a stated subject, "and such rule shall have no force or effect." Drafters fill in the agency and the rule. They add nothing else.

That fixed text sets the resolution apart from an ordinary bill. Lawmakers do not draft new policy language or amend the regulation section by section. They name one rule and reject it.

The design narrows what a resolution can do. It cannot strike some provisions of a regulation and keep others. It cannot substitute different requirements. The only outcome it offers is disapproval of the named rule in full.

That all-or-nothing structure shapes the vote itself. A lawmaker who supports most of a regulation but objects to one provision cannot use the resolution to fix that provision. The choice is to keep the entire rule or cancel the entire rule.

The uniform wording also keeps the debate narrow. Every resolution follows the same template, so there is no policy language to negotiate. The question before each chamber is whether the named rule should stand.

Because the prescribed text names one rule, lawmakers who object to several agency regulations draft a separate resolution for each one. Each of those resolutions then moves through Congress on its own and succeeds or fails on its own.

Timing is the second constraint. A member must introduce the resolution within 60 days of continuous session after Congress receives the rule. The count runs in days of continuous session, not calendar days.

That distinction means the deadline cannot be read off a calendar. The window depends on when Congress formally received the rule. It also depends on how many days Congress remains in continuous session afterward.

The Congressional Review Act gives the Senate a fast track for disapproving agency rules. A discharge petition signed by 30 senators can pull a resolution out of committee. A simple majority can then pass it, and it cannot be filibustered.

That combination sets the Senate path apart from the route most legislation travels. Ordinary bills usually depend on committee action to reach the floor. They can also be held up by extended debate on the floor.

The discharge petition addresses the first obstacle. A committee that does not act on a joint resolution of disapproval cannot keep it from moving. Once 30 senators sign the petition, the measure comes out of committee and heads toward a floor vote.

Floor debate on the resolution is limited under the statute. That limit addresses the second obstacle. Senators who oppose the resolution cannot use open-ended debate to prevent a final vote from taking place.

No supermajority is needed at the final vote. A party holding a narrow Senate majority can pass a disapproval resolution with its own members' votes. Support from the minority party is not required.

The expedited process is available for a limited time. It lasts for 60 Senate session days after the rule is received by Congress and published.

A session day counts only when the Senate actually meets. Recesses and adjournments do not add to the total. As a result, the window can run longer than 60 calendar days.

The count depends on two events, receipt and publication. A rule that has reached Congress but has not yet been published has not started the Senate window.

Once the 60 session days pass, the fast-track procedures no longer apply to that rule. Senators who want to use the discharge petition and the majority-vote threshold must act inside the window.

The Senate fast track governs only one chamber. A disapproval resolution passed this way still needs approval from the House and the president before it becomes law.

A Congressional Review Act resolution must pass the House and the Senate and then win the president's signature to cancel a federal rule. Unlike the Senate, the House gets no statutory fast track for these measures.

The difference between the chambers is written into the law itself. The statute builds an expedited path for the Senate. It provides no matching procedure for the House. A disapproval resolution introduced in the House gets no special protection from the Congressional Review Act.

House leadership fills that gap with its own tools. The usual route is a special rule reported by the Rules Committee. That special rule sets the terms for bringing the disapproval resolution to the floor. As a result, the timing of a House vote rests with the chamber's leadership rather than with a deadline in the statute.

Passage in one chamber is not enough. Both the House and the Senate must approve the same resolution before it reaches the president.

The president then has two choices: sign the resolution or veto it. A signature enacts the disapproval. A veto returns the measure to Congress.

Overriding a veto takes a two-thirds vote in each chamber. Both the House and the Senate must clear that threshold separately. A resolution that passed either chamber by a narrow margin will not reach two-thirds without added support. That math makes a veto difficult to overcome.

The presidential step shapes how the law works in practice. A president is unlikely to approve a resolution that cancels a rule from the president's own administration. Successful use of the Congressional Review Act has mostly come under one set of conditions.

The president and the congressional majorities belong to the same party. The targeted rule came from a predecessor's administration. Under that alignment, both chambers can pass a disapproval resolution and the president can sign it. That pattern links the law's real-world use to changes in party control of the White House.

Once a Congressional Review Act disapproval becomes law, the targeted rule is treated as though it never took effect. The agency that wrote it also loses the power to reissue a rule that is "substantially the same" unless a later law specifically authorizes it.

Those two consequences point in opposite directions in time. One erases the rule's past. The other limits what the agency can do next on the same subject.

The "never took effect" treatment reaches backward. The rule does not simply end on the date the resolution becomes law. For legal purposes, it is handled as if it had never been in force.

The reissue bar reaches forward. An agency cannot return months later with a near-copy of the disapproved rule. To write something substantially similar, it needs Congress to pass a new law that specifically allows it. Like any other legislation, that law must clear both chambers. It must then either win the president's signature or survive a veto.

The open question is the phrase itself. The statute does not define "substantially the same." It offers no test for how far a replacement must depart from the original before it falls outside the ban.

That gap leaves the scope of the restriction unsettled. An agency weighing a new rule on a subject Congress has already disapproved has no statutory definition to consult. It has to judge for itself how close is too close.

The Congressional Review Act adds a further limit. It states that actions and omissions under the law are not subject to judicial review. That language narrows the usual path of asking a court to decide what a statute requires.

The result pairs a firm prohibition with an undefined standard. A disapproved rule cannot come back on the agency's own authority. The boundary between a barred near-copy and a permissible new rule remains unsettled in the statute's text.

The Congressional Review Act's lookback period gives a new session of Congress a chance to review rules that arrived late in the previous one. It applies to rules submitted within 60 legislative days in the House, or 60 session days in the Senate, before sine die adjournment.

Sine die adjournment is the formal end of a session. A chamber adjourns without setting a date to return. The lookback window counts backward from that point.

Rules caught in the window are treated as though they were submitted on the 15th session day of the next session. That legal fiction resets the timing. The review clock starts over in the new session.

The restart reaches the introduction deadline too. Lawmakers have 60 days of continuous session after a rule is received to introduce a joint resolution of disapproval. Under the lookback, that period runs from the 15th session day of the new session.

The Senate's fast-track window restarts as well. Senators get a fresh 60 session days to use the expedited procedure against rules that reached Congress in the final stretch of the prior session.

The window is measured in days a chamber actually meets, not calendar days. The House count uses legislative days. The Senate count uses session days. Each chamber keeps its own calendar, so the two windows can open on different dates.

No one can pin down the start of the lookback window while a session is still under way. It depends on how many days each chamber meets before final adjournment. The start date is fixed only after the fact, once the calendar is complete.

That uncertainty matters for agencies finishing rules late in a session. A rule sent to Congress near the end of a session may or may not fall inside the window. The answer turns on a schedule that has not yet played out.

Rules that arrive earlier in a session do not get this second chance. They follow the ordinary review timeline instead.

Congress first used the Congressional Review Act successfully in 2001, overturning the Occupational Safety and Health Administration's ergonomics rule. Successful use then stayed rare until 2017, when a Republican Congress and President Donald Trump overturned many Obama-era rules.

The ergonomics rule came from OSHA, the workplace safety agency within the Department of Labor. Congress passed a joint resolution of disapproval against it. President George W. Bush signed the measure on March 20, 2001. With his signature, the resolution became law and the rule was canceled.

That first success arrived just under five years after President Bill Clinton signed the CRA on March 29, 1996. For that entire stretch, the statute had not canceled a single regulation.

The disapproval had lasting consequences for OSHA. Under the law, the ergonomics rule is treated as though it never took effect. The agency also cannot issue a rule that is "substantially the same" unless a later act of Congress specifically authorizes it.

For years after 2001, the ergonomics repeal stood as the only successful use of the CRA. Lawmakers kept the power to introduce disapproval resolutions throughout that period.

Use of the statute rose sharply in 2017, 16 years after the ergonomics vote. President Donald Trump took office that year alongside a Republican Congress. Those majorities used the CRA to overturn many rules finalized under the Obama administration.

The 2017 surge fit the conditions under which the CRA has mostly worked. The president belonged to the same party as the congressional majorities and was willing to sign their disapprovals. The rules in question had been issued by a predecessor rather than by the sitting administration.

That alignment matters because the president can veto a disapproval resolution like any other joint resolution. Overriding a veto requires two-thirds of each chamber. Without shared party control, a resolution that clears both chambers can still fail at the president's desk.

How a bill becomes law in the U.S., from introduction to veto override

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

How a bill becomes law in the U.S., from a lawmaker's introduction through committee, floor votes and the president's desk, explained in 10 consecutive steps

The U.S. Constitution gives Congress the power to write federal law, but it also sets strict conditions on that power. Article I, Section 7 lays out a narrow path. A bill has to pass both the House of Representatives and the Senate in identical form. Then it goes to the president, who can sign it, veto it or, in some circumstances, let it take effect without a signature.

Understanding how a bill becomes law helps explain much of what appears in the news about Capitol Hill. A headline about a committee hearing, a stalled vote or a veto threat describes one specific point in a longer sequence. Knowing where that point sits makes it easier to judge what could happen next. It also shows why a proposal that sounds popular can still go nowhere.

The process filters legislation at nearly every stage. Many bills are introduced, referred to a committee and never heard from again. Others clear one chamber and stall in the other. Even a bill that passes both chambers can still be vetoed by the president.

Each stage serves a different purpose. Some stages decide which proposals get attention at all. Others shape the text through amendments and negotiation. A few function as checks, giving one chamber or one branch of government the chance to block what another has approved. The House and Senate also run their floors under different rules. The same bill can move quickly in one chamber and slowly in the other.

The 10 slides that follow walk through the steps in the legislative process in the order a typical bill encounters them. The path starts with a lawmaker formally introducing a bill. It runs through committee work, floor debate in each chamber and the effort to settle differences between the House and Senate versions. It ends with the president's decision and, in some cases, a final vote by Congress to override a veto.

That sequence describes the usual route, not the only one. Congressional procedure includes exceptions that can speed a bill up or let it skip a stage. The Senate can pass measures by unanimous consent. House members can use a discharge petition to pull a bill out of committee. Budget reconciliation runs under its own procedures. Each slide explains one stage on its own terms. A reader can start anywhere in the process.

Every federal bill starts when a sitting member of the House or Senate formally introduces it. That lawmaker becomes the bill's sponsor, the person responsible for it at every later stage.

The idea itself can come from almost anywhere. A president can ask Congress for a new program. A business group, a union, a city government or a citizen advocacy organization can draft language and shop it around Capitol Hill. Executive agencies also send proposed legislation to Congress. None of these outside players can put a bill into the system on their own, though. A lawmaker has to agree to sponsor it.

Drafting usually happens before introduction. Members and their staffs can get help from the Office of the Legislative Counsel. Each chamber maintains its own version of that office to turn policy goals into statutory text. The office works on a nonpartisan basis.

The sponsor can also recruit cosponsors, other members who sign on to show support. Lawmakers who join on the day of introduction are known as original cosponsors. Others can add their names later. A long cosponsor list signals backing, but it does not by itself move the bill forward.

In the House, introduction is a physical act. The member places the bill in the hopper, a wooden box near the rostrum in the House chamber. Members can do this only when the House is in session. In the Senate, a senator typically submits the bill to the clerks at the presiding officer's desk. A senator can also introduce it after being recognized on the floor.

Each bill then gets a number. House bills carry the prefix H.R., and Senate bills carry S. Numbering starts over with each new two-year Congress. That means H.R. 1 in one Congress has nothing to do with H.R. 1 in the next. Any bill not enacted by the time a Congress ends expires. A sponsor who wants to keep pushing it must reintroduce it under a new number.

The Constitution places one limit on where a bill can start. Article I, Section 7 requires that all bills for raising revenue originate in the House of Representatives. The Senate can still propose or concur with amendments to those bills, as it can with any other legislation.

After a bill is introduced, it goes to a committee, where lawmakers assigned to its subject area review it first. In the House, the Speaker refers the bill to one or more committees on the advice of the parliamentarian. In the Senate, the presiding officer makes the referral.

The choice of committee depends on subject matter. Each chamber's rules spell out which committee covers which policy areas. In the House, Rule X $TWTR sets out those jurisdictions. In the Senate, Rule XXV does the same job.

A bill dealing with federal courts or criminal law would typically go to a Judiciary Committee, a panel both chambers have. A House bill on telecommunications or public health might go to the Energy and Commerce Committee. A tax measure in the Senate would usually land at the Finance Committee.

The parliamentarian plays a central role in those decisions. Each chamber has its own parliamentarian, a nonpartisan official who advises on rules and precedents. The parliamentarian's office reviews the text of a new bill and recommends where it belongs. In the Senate, the presiding officer generally follows that recommendation.

A single bill can cover several policy areas at once. The House handles that through multiple referral, which sends the bill to more than one committee. The Speaker usually designates a primary committee and can set time limits for the others. The Senate typically sends a bill to the one committee whose jurisdiction covers most of its subject matter. Senate Rule XIV also lets a senator skip referral and place a bill directly on the Senate calendar.

Referral shapes what happens next. A committee chair largely controls the panel's agenda. That means the chair has wide say over whether a bill receives any attention.

Most bills go no further than committee. A committee can simply take no action on a measure, and that inaction alone can end its chances. A bill that is still pending when a two-year Congress ends expires. Its sponsor can reintroduce it in the next Congress. The new version receives a new bill number and starts over with a fresh committee referral.

A committee often hands a newly referred bill to one of its subcommittees for closer study. Lawmakers there may hold hearings with witnesses, then meet for a markup session where they propose, debate and vote on amendments to the bill's actual wording.

Full committees cover broad subject areas, and subcommittees split that work into smaller pieces. A House panel such as Energy and Commerce handles policy ranging from health care to telecommunications. It divides that territory among smaller groups of members. Each subcommittee works on a narrower slice of policy and builds expertise in it.

The subcommittee chair has wide control over the schedule. The chair decides whether to hold a hearing, which bills get attention and in what order they move. Members of the minority party can request witnesses and offer amendments, but the majority sets the agenda.

Hearings are the fact-finding stage. Witnesses can include officials from federal agencies, academic specialists, industry representatives, advocacy groups and people directly affected by a proposal. Witnesses usually submit written testimony in advance and then answer questions from members. The hearing record becomes part of the bill's paper trail. Hearings are not required, and some bills go straight to markup without one.

Markup is where the text itself changes. The session starts from a base text. That text may be the bill as introduced, or a substitute version offered by the chair. Members then offer amendments section by section. Each amendment is debated and decided by a vote of the members present. Those votes can be taken by voice or recorded by name.

Amendments range from small fixes, such as changing a word or a date, to rewrites of entire sections. A markup can also adopt an amendment in the nature of a substitute. That device replaces the bill's whole text with a new version in a single vote.

When a subcommittee finishes its markup, it can vote to forward the bill to the full committee for further action. Practice varies across the House and Senate. Some committees skip the subcommittee step entirely and hold hearings and markup at the full-committee level instead.

A bill typically reaches the House or Senate floor only after the full committee votes to report it. That vote usually comes with a written committee report explaining what the bill does and why. A bill the committee never reports usually dies there.

The reporting vote follows markup. Committee members decide by majority vote whether to send the measure to the full chamber. They can attach the amendments adopted during markup. A committee may also bundle those changes into a single amendment in the nature of a substitute. That amendment replaces the original text with a rewritten version.

A committee can report a bill favorably, unfavorably or without recommendation. A favorable report is the common outcome.

The committee chair controls much of the committee's schedule. A chair who does not take up a bill can keep it from ever reaching a reporting vote.

The committee report is a public document with its own number, labeled H. Rept. in the House and S. Rept. in the Senate. It typically describes the bill's purpose, summarizes its provisions and lays out the changes it would make to existing law. Members who disagree with the majority can add minority or additional views. In the House, reports generally include a cost estimate from the Congressional Budget Office. Courts and federal agencies sometimes consult these reports later to understand what lawmakers intended.

Once reported, a bill goes onto a calendar of pending business. The House uses the Union Calendar for most bills that raise or spend money and the House Calendar for many others. The Senate places reported bills on its Calendar of Business.

House members have one formal way around a committee that will not act. A discharge petition can pull a bill out of committee once 218 members sign it, a majority of the full 435-seat House. The petition can be filed only after the bill has sat in committee for 30 legislative days. Signatures on discharge petitions have been public since 1993.

A bill that stays in committee does not wait indefinitely. Any measure not enacted by the end of a two-year Congress expires. Its supporters must reintroduce it in the next Congress for it to be considered again.

Before most major bills reach the House floor, the House Rules Committee writes a special rule that decides how long members can debate and whether they can offer amendments. The full House must vote to adopt that rule before debate on the bill begins.

This step has no counterpart in the Senate. It places a formal gatekeeper between committee approval and a floor vote in the House.

A special rule takes the form of a House resolution, numbered with the prefix H.Res. It typically sets a fixed block of debate time and divides that time between the bill's supporters and opponents. It can also waive procedural objections, known as points of order, that might otherwise stall the measure.

The rule's amendment terms come in three main forms. An open rule lets any member offer an amendment that is germane to the bill. A structured rule allows only the amendments the Rules Committee lists in its report. A closed rule bars floor amendments entirely, so the House votes on the bill largely as written.

Members who want changes usually submit their proposed amendments to the Rules Committee in advance. The committee then decides which ones, if any, will reach the floor. That decision determines which changes the full House can consider at all.

The majority party's leadership chooses the majority's members of the committee. That arrangement gives the Speaker significant influence over which bills move and on what terms. Votes to adopt a rule usually break along party lines. If a rule fails, the bill cannot come up under those terms, and debate on it does not begin.

The House typically debates the rule itself for one hour before voting on it.

Some bills skip the Rules Committee altogether. Under a procedure called suspension of the rules, the House limits debate to 40 minutes and permits no floor amendments. Passage under suspension requires a two-thirds vote of members present and voting.

A bill passes the House when a simple majority of members present and voting supports it, as long as a quorum is on hand. Floor debate follows the terms of the rule the chamber adopted for that bill. A winning vote sends the measure to the Senate.

The Constitution defines a House quorum as a majority of members. With all 435 seats filled, that is 218. Much of the amending work happens in the Committee of the Whole House on the state of the Union. This procedural device lets the full membership act with a quorum of 100.

Debate time is usually split between two floor managers, one from the majority and one from the minority. They are often leaders of the committee that handled the bill. Each manager yields minutes to colleagues who want to speak. The rule controls how long this lasts and which amendments, if any, members may offer.

Votes can be taken by voice, by division or by recorded vote. The Constitution lets one-fifth of members present demand that the yeas and nays be entered in the journal. Recorded votes have used an electronic system since 1973. Members insert voting cards into stations on the floor. The first vote in a series stays open for at least 15 minutes.

Before final passage, the minority can offer a motion to recommit. If adopted, it sends the bill back to committee. A tie vote defeats the question. Passage requires more yes votes than no votes.

Not every bill travels this route. Measures considered under suspension of the rules skip a special rule. They allow no floor amendments and get 40 minutes of debate. In exchange, passage requires two-thirds of members present and voting.

Once the House approves a bill, clerks prepare an engrossed copy that reflects every adopted amendment. That certified text goes to the Senate as an act of the House. A bill that began in the Senate reaches the House only after Senate passage. It then moves through these same House steps.

A bill on the Senate floor can pass with a simple majority, but ending debate on it usually takes 60 votes. As a result, the cloture vote, not the final passage vote, is often the harder threshold for legislation to clear in the U.S. Senate.

Senate debate is generally open-ended. Unless senators agree to limits, a bill can stay on the floor as long as members keep talking or keep procedural objections alive. Using that freedom to delay or block a final vote is called a filibuster.

Limits on debate often come through unanimous consent agreements. These agreements can set how long debate runs and which amendments receive votes. A single senator's objection can keep such an agreement from taking effect.

When consent fails, supporters of a bill turn to cloture, the formal process for ending debate under Senate Rule XXII. A cloture motion must carry the signatures of 16 senators. The vote on it takes place one hour after the Senate convenes on the second day after the motion is filed.

Invoking cloture on most legislation requires three-fifths of senators duly chosen and sworn. With all 100 seats filled, that equals 60 votes. The threshold is measured against sitting senators rather than those present. An absent senator therefore offers no help to the side trying to reach 60.

A successful cloture vote does not end consideration on the spot. Rule XXII allows up to 30 more hours of consideration before the final vote. Passage itself then needs only a simple majority of senators voting. If that vote ties, the vice president, serving as president of the Senate, can cast the deciding vote under Article I, Section 3 of the Constitution.

The cloture rule dates to 1917. For decades, it required two-thirds of senators present and voting. In 1975, the Senate replaced that standard with the current three-fifths of senators duly chosen and sworn.

Budget reconciliation bills are an exception to this process. They move under limited debate time and cannot be filibustered. They therefore do not need 60 votes to reach a final vote.

Both chambers of Congress must pass the exact same text before a bill can go to the president. When the House and Senate approve different versions, lawmakers have three ways to close the gap. One chamber can accept the other's text, the chambers can trade amendments, or a conference committee can negotiate a compromise.

Acceptance is the most direct route. If one chamber is willing to take the other's version as written, it passes that text. The two chambers are then in agreement, and no further negotiation is needed.

The second route is an exchange of amendments. One chamber amends the other's bill and sends it back. The receiving chamber can agree, propose its own amendment to that amendment, or insist on its position. This back-and-forth is informally known as "ping-pong." It lets leaders settle differences without appointing a formal negotiating panel.

The third route is a conference committee. This is a temporary panel made up of members from both the House and the Senate. The Speaker names the House conferees, and the Senate's presiding officer appoints the Senate conferees. Conferees usually come from the committees that handled the bill.

Conferees from each chamber vote as a separate delegation. A compromise moves forward only when a majority of the House conferees and a majority of the Senate conferees sign the conference report. That report contains the agreed text. A joint explanatory statement accompanies it and describes how the negotiators resolved each difference.

Both chambers then vote on the conference report. Neither the House nor the Senate can amend it on the floor. Members accept or reject it as a whole. If either chamber votes it down, the disagreement stays open. The bill cannot go to the president in that form.

Once the two chambers agree on identical text by any of these routes, the chamber where the bill started prepares a final official copy. That copy is called the enrolled bill. The Speaker of the House and the presiding officer of the Senate sign it. Only then is the measure ready to be sent to the White House.

Once Congress presents a bill, the president has 10 days, Sundays excepted, to act on it. A signature makes the bill law, a veto sends it back, and inaction can produce either outcome depending on whether Congress is still in session.

That deadline comes from Article I, Section 7 of the U.S. Constitution. The clock starts when the bill is presented to the president, not when the final floor vote takes place. Before presentment, the identical text approved by both chambers is prepared as an enrolled bill. The Speaker of the House and the presiding officer of the Senate sign it before it goes to the White House.

Signing is the most direct route. With the president's signature, the measure becomes law. Presidents sometimes sign major legislation at public ceremonies. The legal effect comes from the signature itself, not the event around it.

A regular veto takes a different path. The president returns the bill to the chamber where it originated, along with the president's objections. The Constitution directs that chamber to enter those objections in its journal and reconsider the measure. The president acts on the bill as a whole and cannot approve some provisions while rejecting others.

A president can also decline to act. If 10 days pass, Sundays excepted, while Congress is in session, the bill becomes law without a signature. In that situation, the president's silence counts as acceptance rather than rejection.

The outcome changes when Congress has adjourned. If lawmakers have left and the president does not sign within the window, the bill does not become law. This is called a pocket veto. The Constitution's text describes it as a case in which Congress, by its adjournment, prevents the bill's return.

The difference between the two kinds of veto shapes what happens next. A regular veto puts the bill and the president's objections back in front of Congress, which can try to reverse the decision. A pocket veto leaves no returned bill for lawmakers to reconsider. Supporters of a measure blocked this way have to introduce it again as a new bill and restart the process.

A presidential veto does not have to be the final word on a bill. Congress can override it if two-thirds of each chamber votes to pass the bill again. That is a far higher bar than the simple majority needed the first time.

The Constitution spells out the override procedure. The vetoed bill returns to the chamber where it started, along with the president's objections. That chamber records the objections in its journal and reconsiders the bill. If two-thirds agree to pass it, the bill moves to the other chamber. The second chamber must also reach two-thirds.

The Constitution requires override votes to be taken by yeas and nays. Each member's name and vote are entered in that chamber's journal. Every override attempt is therefore a recorded vote. There is no option for an anonymous voice vote.

If every seat is filled and every member votes, an override takes 290 of 435 House members and 67 of 100 senators. The threshold is measured against members present and voting, provided a quorum is present. The exact number can shift with absences and vacancies. The Supreme Court upheld that reading in Missouri Pacific Railway Co. v. Kansas.

If the first chamber falls short, the veto stands. The second chamber then has nothing to vote on. Overrides are rare. A bill that clears both override votes becomes law over the president's objections.

Once a bill is enacted, whether by signature or by override, it receives a public law number. The first part of that number identifies the Congress that passed it. The second part reflects the order in which it was enacted during that Congress.

The new law is first issued as a slip law, a standalone printed copy of the single measure. It is later compiled in the Statutes at Large, the permanent chronological record of the laws Congress passes. Laws of general and permanent effect are then folded into the U.S. Code, which arranges federal law by subject. The Office of the Law Revision Counsel of the House handles that codification.

Man Utd Make Jason Wilcox and Omar Berrada Sack Decision

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Manchester United are believed to have already reached their verdict not just on the future of Michael Carrick, but also regarding two other key higher-ups, Omar Berrada and Jason Wilcox, who have also received serious scrutiny over recent months.

With multiple outlets now reporting that the former Man Utd midfielder turned interim boss and now full-time head coach is set to be relieved of his duties in the very near future – and with United's preferred candidate to replace Michael Carrick already named – pundits, supporters, and football fans in general are debating where exactly the book stops at the giant of a sporting brand, as countless have questioned where the true accountability for their poor fortunes in the post-Fergie era lies.

Berrada and Wilcox, both of whom found success on the business side of things during their time at Man City, are now the latest to be targeted by chants around Old Trafford and protest banners hanging from stands in the Stretford End.

Manchester United chiefs are expected to sack Michael Carrick

Nevertheless, while they're still trying to back their manager and thank him for his efforts during one of the most difficult periods in the club's long history, GIVEMESPORT understands that the pair are safe. At least for now, anyway.

As per The Telegraph's James Ducker and other sources, the executive duo — Berrada serving as CEO and Wilcox as Director of Football, respectively — are expected to keep their jobs if/when Carrick is dismissed; it's said they maintain co-owner Sir Jim Ratcliffe's support as part of a core triumvirate.

Since INEOS' investment in Manchester United, they have already shuffled significantly not only when it comes to the backroom staff but within the boardroom too, but it looks as though he still considers them key advisers and pillars of the so-called 'Project 2028' rebuild.

Ratcliffe has reiterated that, despite stating that he doesn't mind being "unpopular" and making the tough decisions, the increasing levels of abuse he and his cohort have received over the course of their tenure at the club, stating that he would walk away from the whole franchise if it ever became severe and prolonged as the anti-ownership sentiment and demonstrations the Glazer family have seen.

On the one hand, Wilcox's involvement in footballing decisions has been perceived as an issue by many Reds (this being the ex-player's first role of this kind), with divisive defender Lisandro Martínez publicly criticising the sporting director's decision-making in his highly inflammatory post-match quotes following their Champions League defeat to Atlético Madrid.

Meanwhile, more senior 'leadership' figures such as the World Cup winner himself have equally come under fire for sub-par performances of late, making key match-deciding errors and, crucially, irrespective of whatever big statements made in high-profile interviews, not doing enough talking on the pitch.

For most United match-goers and fans who watch it with their eyes week in, week out, whether or not Carrick stays or goes won't be enough to fix the deep-seated problems running throughout Manchester United; they'd much prefer wholesale change from the very top of the hierarchy all the way down - though it's likely they'll be waiting a long time to see that on a big enough scale to quell their frustrations.

'Green gold until the club is sold', as the supporters' saying goes, but do any of you really foresee the Glazers selling up one of the biggest sporting franchises in the world anytime soon?

In the age of AI, Barbara Tfank is, refreshingly, all about craft. Unlike other designers who may have moved on, she’s stayed true to her passion for eveningwear. As such, she continues to seek out fabrics from European textile mills she’s used since day one. This season, she looked to her vast archive and found a few unused Bianchini Férier prints to repurpose for her spring collection. “The artist Raoul Dufy actually painted and designed the textiles for them, so they’re collectibles,” she explained of the lively, lush florals in vivid hues. “I was just so happy to see these in my archive because there’s nothing digital about them, it was all done by hand and just feels timeless.”

With the prints, she revamped some beloved Tfank styles like her signature off-the-shoulder gowns in new spring-ready organza. Along those same lines, she explored a few casual styles, like the bra tops that have been everywhere on spring 2027 runways, hers made in a groovy Férier print and paired with wide-leg pants. Other options include an airy halterneck dress with a long train and a casual navy-and-white sundress, for which Tfank mentions the fabric was once used to create a custom coat for First Lady Michelle Obama. Talk about divine timing to bring the print back.

CoreWeave beats on revenue and lifts guidance, easing AI slowdown concerns

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

CoreWeave reported better-than-expected quarterly revenue and lifted its 2026 guidance on Tuesday, showing that demand for artificial intelligence infrastructure remains strong despite concerns of a market slowdown. The stock advanced in extended trading.

The earnings announcement lands at a particularly volatile moment for CoreWeave and its neocloud peers, as the market sours on the AI trade and as Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, the central figure in the global buildout, faces a scandal tied to an affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

Here's how the company performed relative to LSEG consensus:

CoreWeave's revenue soared 168% from about $1.36 billion a year earlier, according to a statement. The company recorded a net loss of $709 million, or $1.27 per share, compared to a net loss of $110 million a year earlier. Adjusted earnings exclude stock-based compensation.

Executives pushed up their forecast for 2026. They now see $1.275 billion to $1.375 billion in adjusted operating income on $13 billion to $13.3 billion in revenue. Analysts surveyed by Visible Alpha were looking for $1.07 billion in adjusted operating income, and analysts polled by LSEG had expected $12.86 billion in 2026 revenue.

In August, CoreWeave projected $960 million to $1.15 billion in adjusted operating income and $12.4 billion to $13.2 billion in revenue.

CoreWeave has lost over 40% of its value in the past month, as investors rotate out of companies most closely tied to AI infrastructure. Rising interest rates have pushed lenders out of the market, raising concerns about ongoing access to capital. Some CoreWeave bond yields recently topped 16%.

"The business can execute well and still face a tougher financing environment," Joe Tigay, portfolio manager at Equity Armor Investments, wrote in an email. "Strong demand is encouraging; the cost and availability of capital remain critical."

CoreWeave will get most the capital it needs for 2027 through clients pre-payments and financing from hardware partners, and the company will raise "a limited amount" as well, CEO Mike Intrator told analysts on a conference call.

The company plans to raise new debt tied to a set of contracts in the weeks to come, said Nitin Agrawal, its finance chief.

"At current yields, we do not intend to issue additional parent-level unsecured debt through 2027," he said. "By contrast, the asset-level secured debt market has remained stable, with our loans continuing to trade around par value."

Fellow neocloud Nebius, whose stock is down by a similar amount, is scheduled to report earnings on Thursday. Nebius shares got a small boost from CoreWeave's results.

Both companies and several other providers of cloud-based graphics processing units are particularly reliant on Nvidia, which provides GPUs as well as necessary capital to the whole ecosystem. Nvidia shares have dropped nearly 10% to start the week as the Huang affair raises pressure on the company in Washington, D.C. Nvidia's board said on Tuesday that it hired Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to investigate Huang and determine whether national security or company secrets were compromised.

CoreWeave investors are also looking toward next week, when key customer Anthropic is scheduled to debut on the Nasdaq. In its updated IPO prospectus on Tuesday, Anthropic indicated that it could be valued at around $1.4 trillion after its share sale.

During the quarter, CoreWeave said it would establish its first data centers in Asia and announced the Forge service for running and evaluating AI models.

Management still anticipates 1.85 gigawatts in active power by the end of 2026. CoreWeave is targeting $37 billion to $39 billion in full-year capital expenditures, which would imply 155% growth at the middle of the range. Analysts polled by Visible Alpha were looking for $37.69 billion.

CoreWeave owed almost $47 billion in debt at the end of the third quarter, according to the statement. Like its bigger cloud rivals such as Amazon and Oracle, CoreWeave is rushing to bolster computing capacity for clients. But last week, voters in the U.S., where CoreWeave derives most of its revenue, rejected dozens of initiatives on data center development.

"If data center construction across the sector were to slow, installed compute becomes scarcer, and the knock-on effect is that GPU pricing may stay higher for longer," said Intrator, a CoreWeave co-founder.

He said the company does not expect risks to deliveries in 2027 based on construction at permitted locations.

"We could sell all of the capacity that we have to current buyers that are in the queue. We are choosing not to," Intrator said. We're choosing to be patient. We're choosing to bring the infrastructure to market when it is further along, because we believe that that will result in better economic output for the outstanding infrastructure that we have."

Meanwhile, demand remains robust, with the company now sitting on a $132.5 billion revenue backlog, up from $104 billion as of June.

"Should we choose to focus only on our committed backlog, we would expect capex for 2027 to remain in line with our 2026 capex investment, while revenue and EBITDA grow significantly," Agrawal said.

OpenAI Quarterly Revenue Rises About 50% as Operating Loss Widens

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Reuters reported on November 10, 2026 that OpenAI generated about $10 billion in third-quarter revenue, an increase of roughly 50%, citing the Wall Street Journal’s review of investor documents. Its operating loss nevertheless widened to $10.9 billion from $10.1 billion.

The reported adjusted operating margin improved to negative 109% from negative 150% in the prior quarter. That improvement did not prevent a larger dollar operating loss, leaving better margins alongside a deeper absolute loss. OpenAI did not immediately respond to Reuters’ request for comment.

OpenAI has made compute central to its account of the business. In a January 18, 2026 article, CFO Sarah Friar said annualized revenue exceeded $20 billion in 2025 alongside approximately 1.9 gigawatts of available compute.

Friar described revenue as directly tracking available compute and said additional capacity in earlier periods would have accelerated customer adoption and monetization. The business model spans consumer and workplace subscriptions, usage-based APIs, and a free tier supported by advertising and commerce. In that cycle, compute supports research and model capability, stronger models improve products and adoption, and adoption generates revenue to finance further compute.

Compute commitments must be made years in advance, with capital deployed in tranches against demand signals and infrastructure supplied through partnerships rather than ownership. Friar acknowledged that capacity sometimes leads usage and usage sometimes leads capacity. The approach provides flexibility across providers and hardware, but it also makes the timing of investment consequential because demand and capacity do not necessarily expand together.

OpenAI’s April 8, 2026 enterprise update said enterprise customers generated more than 40% of revenue and were on track to reach parity with consumer revenue by the end of 2026. ChatGPT had 900 million weekly users. New enterprise customers included Goldman Sachs, Phillips and State Farm, while usage was growing at existing customers including Cursor, DoorDash, Thermo Fisher and LY Corporation. Those disclosures describe revenue opportunities across both mass consumer adoption and organizational deployments.

OpenAI’s March 31, 2026 funding announcement said it had closed a round with $122 billion in committed capital at an $852 billion post-money valuation and presented durable compute access as the basis for research advances, better products, wider access and lower delivery costs at scale. Its infrastructure portfolio at the time spanned cloud providers Microsoft, Oracle, AWS, CoreWeave and Google Cloud, plus chip and data-center partners. Nvidia remained the foundation of the training fleet and the majority of its inference stack.

The reported quarter puts OpenAI’s commercial scale and operating burden side by side. Rapid sales growth, a substantial enterprise business and committed capital provide the basis for expansion, but the widening loss shows that revenue has not yet caught up with operating costs. The central issue for future disclosures is whether adoption can translate into a smaller operating loss while OpenAI maintains the compute capacity behind its products.

Fossil CEO says turnaround is ahead of plan as sales return to growth

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Fossil Group posted quarterly results on Tuesday that could point to early signs of a turnaround for the legacy watchmaker.

The company, which has struggled to compete with new gadgets in the years since its sales and stock price peaked in the 2010s, has been quietly undertaking a comeback strategy under CEO Franco Fogliato. The company's fiscal third-quarter earnings indicate the strategy is beginning to bear fruit.

The company said Tuesday that it returned to profitability in the third quarter, posting operating income of $25.3 million, compared with an operating loss of $21.7 million last year. It also returned to sales growth, with net sales up 0.4% to $271.2 million. The company last reported a year-over-year sales increase in the first fiscal quarter of 2022.

"We're excited about improving dramatically the profitability of the company through a very strong and disciplined management of full-price sales as well as cost management," Fogliato told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

For the quarter ended Oct. 3, Fossil reported net income of $11.7 million, or 19 cents per share, compared with a net loss of $39.9 million, or 76 cents per share, the year prior. The company also said its operating expenses fell 11.1% to $137 million. The company last posted quarterly net income in the third fiscal quarter of 2022.

With the signs of growth, the retailer also raised its full-year outlook, guiding for full-year net sales of between $980 million and $990 million, which would mark a slight decline from just over $1 billion in net sales in the prior year. That compares with a previous expectation of net sales falling between 3% and 5%. Fossil also said it's expecting positive free cash flow by the end of the year.

Fogliato said Fossil had previously not expected sales growth for another year and a half.

The stronger quarter comes after the company saw slumping sales and financial losses over the past few years. It struggled with the rise of smartwatches, mounting debt and the decline in mall foot traffic.

Fossil stock has lost the vast majority of its value since the early-to-mid 2010s, and despite a recent surge, was trading under $8 a share on Tuesday.

To rightsize the business, Fogliato, who joined Fossil in 2024, said he focused on closing stores, moving away from promotions to full-price sales, improving marketing and broadening Fossil's product range. That meant shrinking the business to a sustainable baseline before trying to grow it.

The company saw a 5.9% decrease in direct-to-consumer sales for its third quarter, which Fogliato attributed to the company's "aggressive" downsizing.

"We simplified a lot of the business doing this," Fogliato said, adding that customers responded well when the company moved away from markdowns. "People tell me they are excited about what they are seeing in the marketplace now."

Fossil added that its key growth engine for the quarter was Asia, where net sales grew 15.1%. Fogliato said much of that growth was led by the India market.

At the same time, growing internationally is only part of the watchmaker's strategy as it tries to improve its performance in its home country.

"Priority number one is to grow our home market, and we're doing that," he said. "As retail is settling into a smaller, more profitable base, ready to grow again, we're entering 2027 with plenty of opportunities."

Fogliato said one key to growth was not trying to keep up with the smartwatch trend. While trying to make gains in the smartwatch space initially hampered Fossil's performance, it's now investing in analog watches again.

Smartwatch sales came in under $1 million for the quarter, but sales for traditional watches — which make up around 86% of Fossil's sales — grew 6.4%.

"Fossil is literally having a comeback, a strong comeback, a momentum, and we can see this," Fogliato said. "And it comes not only through the numbers — it comes with the excitement we see in the stores, and we think we're just at the beginning. We have so much work to do."

Reddit’s New AI-Powered Reviews Are Already Controversial

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Tech > Services & Software > Online > Online Services

The platform's test of automated product review summaries is sparking concern that advertising will replace human advice.

Reddit announced on Tuesday that it’s testing Reviews, an AI-powered shopping feature that aggregates user comments into product summaries, commerce links, and pros-and-cons breakdowns. In a blog post, the company said it wants to organically use discussions on the platform, from conversations about skin care treatments or coffee to headphones, to help people “understand what a product is really like and feel confident deciding whether it’s right for them.”

The Reviews feature promises to make product research easier on a site that’s increasingly focused on shopping recommendations. But the announcement immediately rankled some Reddit users who are concerned it will degrade authentic human-to-human discussion and advice. Many Reddit moderators, or “mods,” tasked with keeping order of communities called subreddits, believe it’ll be hard to distinguish real opinions from straight-up advertising.

According to Reddit, the upcoming feature will surface product reviews in search results and through highlighted product mentions in comment threads, regardless of a group’s rules preventing the promotion of products by users or brands.

The test launch of Reviews, which was previewed in February and focused on electronics, will start with about 10,000 products across electronics, health and beauty, home and garden, and fashion and apparel. If the experiment works, Reddit plans to expand the feature outside the US and also increase the range of categories and products.

In a Reddit community for the moderators who enforce platform and subreddit rules, the new AI-powered shopping feature was greeted with skepticism and criticism. Writing in modnews, ProudnotLoud dubbed early tests “an ad-ridden, distracting mess” that made reading threads nearly impossible.

For moderators who work to keep commercial spam out of their subreddits, the feature poses an existential threat. User nerdshark warned that communities are already being actively targeted by stealth corporate campaigns, arguing that forcing Reviews into these spaces “does vulnerable communities a huge disservice,” especially without an opt-out mechanism.

Some Reddit admins have pushed back against the backlash, clarifying that the embedded product links are search shortcuts rather than paid placements. Yet users remain unconvinced. As CouncilofStrongs countered: “From a user experience, it is an ad.”

Reddit has also recently been testing advertising in search results, which could be a way for brands to reach Reddit users who click a product name even if they aren’t paying for placement in Reddit Reviews.

Given the amount of sites that focus heavily on human-centered reviews (you’re reading one of them), Reddit’s intent to aggregate product information through AI summaries could be seen as another way for traditional media publishers to have their content used without compensation.

But Reddit maintains the feature is purely informational. In a statement to CNET, a Reddit spokesperson said that Reviews relies exclusively on internal platform content and “does not source, scrape or republish third-party content.” The company said that because each review links to the original discussion, users on the platform “can explore the context and go deeper. “

Reddit also said that brands can’t buy ads to get into Reddit Reviews. “What appears in Reddit Reviews is shaped by Reddit communities, not paid placement,” the spokesperson said.

However, that reliance on organic Reddit activity brings its own vulnerabilities. One challenge Reddit may face with Reviews is trying to keep its AI focused on authentic human-written reviews versus posts that might come from marketers, coordinated astroturfing or AI-generated spam. Because the platform operates on anonymity, it’s facing an uphill battle with a massive influx of AI bots that could try to game content in Reviews.

The new feature comes alongside Reddit’s recent changes to the platform to detect spam or other manipulated content and keep bots out, including discontinuing support for RSS feeds and limiting access to its content API. The company is also restricting Old Reddit, a previous design version of the platform that has fewer ads.

A Reddit spokesperson told CNET that the platform has “the least amount of AI slop relative to other[s]” and that it is taking “numerous steps to prevent and enforce against inauthentic content.”

But while Reddit insists that Reviews will be centered on personal experience and uncorrupted by advertising dollars, many are worried the feature could blur the line between community research and automated e-commerce.

White House vows to press ahead with tariffs despite midterm backlash over rising prices

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

One week after voters punished Republicans over rising prices in the midterm elections, President Donald Trump's White House signaled it would stay the course on tariffs, even as his former top economic advisor warned that Americans are footing the bill.

Jim Goyer, Trump's director of White House Office of Public Liaison, defended tariffs as a tool to rebuild U.S. manufacturing, secure supply chains and protect national security during an address Tuesday at the B20 summit, a gathering of global business leaders in Washington hosted by the U.S. Chamber of Commerce.

"And we are just getting started," Goyer said. "Our ambition is to continue this pace for the remainder of the president's term in office."

But Gary Cohn, who served as Trump's chief economic advisor during his first administration, offered a sharply different assessment during an onstage interview later Tuesday.

"Tariffs are a consumption tax on the U.S. consumer," Cohn said, arguing that the duties are raising prices and forcing Americans to reconsider purchases.

The divide exposed a growing dilemma for Trump. His trade agenda is reshaping global commerce – and driving up costs for voters. Companies are adapting to the chaos, but the old trading system isn't coming back.

"President Trump has disrupted that system in a way that we're not likely to return to," Kevin Brady, a former Republican congressman who helped lead the B20 summit, said during a discussion with U.S. Chamber CEO Suzanne Clark.

The political backlash was evident in the Nov. 3 election. Roughly two-thirds of voters said Trump's policies contributed to inflation, according to the CNBC Voter Poll. Nearly nine in 10 said the cost of living was a major or crucial factor in their vote.

A separate Reuters/Ipsos poll released Tuesday found 62% of Americans supported repealing Trump's tariffs, while just 21% approved of his handling of the cost of living.

"Last week, American voters sent a clear message about the cost of living and their economic security," Clark said in her keynote address.

The pressure intensified Tuesday as new government data showed consumer prices rose 0.4% in October, exceeding economists' expectations. Annual inflation reached 3.7% in September, the latest available year-over-year figure and well above the Federal Reserve's 2% target.

Tariffs are not the only factor driving higher prices. The war with Iran has pushed energy costs sharply higher, while housing, healthcare and other household expenses continue to strain consumers.

But research has found Trump's import duties have added to those pressures. A Federal Reserve Bank of New York study estimated that about 26% of tariff increases were passed through to consumer prices, with some effects taking nine to 12 months to work through supply chains.

Cohn, now vice chairman of IBM, argued that while tariffs generate revenue for the U.S., those gains come at consumers' expense.

"You're looking at one side of the equation. You're looking at the credit side of the balance sheet," Cohn said, describing how he would respond to Trump's defense of tariff revenue. "There's the debit side of the balance sheet, which is every hardworking American who happened to pay the tariff through some product that they bought."

Still, Cohn saw room for a bipartisan trade deal. He suggested Trump and the incoming Democratic Congress could make protections against China and unfair trade practices permanent while rolling back tariffs on Canada, leaving less for future presidents to undo.

For businesses, the shift is already underway. UPS CEO Carol Tomé said customers are moving production and sourcing closer to home as global supply chains become more regional.

"Supply chains are diversifying. Supply chains are regionalizing," Tomé said during a panel discussion moderated by CNBC's Morgan Brennan.

Separately, UPS is opening a new $80 million-plus air hub in the Philippines in October to expand its Asian network and improve connections across more than 1,100 global trade lanes.

Other executives, like Jim Fitterling, executive chairman of chemicals giant Dow, defended Trump's tariffs despite their costs. Fitterling acknowledged they can drive up prices but argued they are necessary to combat unfair competition and protect U.S. manufacturing.

Democrats plan to challenge Trump's tariffs when they take control of Congress in January, but his veto power and broad trade authority could make them difficult to overturn.

Pas tendre Zoumana !

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Allez Paillade, le site non officiel dédié à l'actualité du Montpellier HSC revient sur la surprenante défaite à domicile des Héraultais contre le FC Metz (2-3) et relate les propos du coach Zoumana Camara, pas tendre avec ses joueurs qui ont laissé filer une occasion de rester dans la roue des Verts :

"Je suis déçu. La leçon, c’est qu’un match se joue 90 minutes. On a été absent des débats sur les 45 premières minutes. Et même sur cette deuxième mi-temps où on est revenu avec de meilleures intentions. Quand on met ce 2-1, on ne doit pas encaisser ce troisième but. On a le sentiment d’avoir fait le plus dur, de pouvoir emballer le match et de créer le doute. Et derrière, on prend un troisième but quelques minutes après. Le sentiment que rien ne tourne dans le bon sens dans cette soirée.

L’absence d’agressivité, dans l’intensité, dans le rythme. Quand on ne met pas ces ingrédients-là, on tombe face à une équipe où il y avait un tout. Une équipe qui a pris un sévère revers à la maison, qui vient de perdre dans la semaine son président historique. Et puis un ancien joueur de la maison qui revient. Donc vous mettez tout ça ensemble, ça crée un esprit et une équipe qui est venue en mode commando, en mode combat avec l’envie de faire quelque chose. Et si nous, on n’a pas la capacité à avoir au moins cette même envie, cette même agressivité, cette même détermination avant de parler du jeu, on ne peut pas exister dans ce championnat et on n’a pas existé aujourd’hui, en tout cas sur la première période.

(Un relâchement ?) Peut-être. J’ai des jeunes joueurs, on est en train d’apprendre un peu dans tout. C’est l’avenir qui dira si c’est un accident ou pas. Mais en tout cas, on était absent des débats aujourd’hui, surtout sur la première mi-temps.

(Un coup d’arrêt ?) Une saison, c’est long. C’est très très long. L’environnement était euphorique. Et parfois ultra-enthousiaste. J’ai dû la saison passée venir beaucoup de fois ici. On parlait d’être un peu plus réaliste face à la situation. Aujourd’hui, les résultats faisaient qu’il y avait cette euphorie. Et c’est juste à juste titre. C’est à nous de devoir, à l’intérieur, canaliser ça, garder les pieds sur terre, continuer à avancer et continuer à mettre les ingrédients qui ont fait la réussite. Et là, on ne les a pas mis ce soir."

Pixel 11 Pro’s HiLight Will Now Light Up When Your Besties Text You

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Google's new LED light is starting to flash for more than just phone calls.

Google’s HiLight LED is starting to light up for more than just phone calls and Gemini on the Pixel 11 Pro, Pro XL and Pro Fold. The multicolored light will now glow when your preferred contacts text you in Google Messages, as part of a new Pixel Drop update announced on Tuesday.

I was pretty harsh about HiLight when it debuted on Google’s higher-cost phones as a “Pro” feature. While it hearkens back to the days of the BlackBerry notification light, which is a good thing, its limitation to just lighting up in one of five colors for your preferred contacts’ phone calls or a color swirl for the Gemini assistant made the feature’s introduction feel half-baked. Third-party apps that offered way more control over the LED made this even more glaring, including support for more colors along with app-specific customizations.

Google representatives at the launch of the Pixel 11 did say that HiLight will continue to get new features, and adding text message support is the right step toward making the light more useful. Hopefully, in time this will expand to other features that the BlackBerry of years gone would light up for, like email and app notifications.

Tuesday’s Pixel Drop also brings several of the new features that debuted on the Pixel 11 over to last year’s Pixel 10, such as Rambler for improving voice dictation and Gemini Intelligence for proactive suggestions based on information shown on your screen. For that latter feature, this should mean quick links to a feature like Google Maps when discussing dinner plans with a friend.

Google’s also gearing up to release the Pixel Tag item tracker on Wednesday and announced that Google Fi is adding multi-network support to improve cellular connections.
