Lumbra Raises $25M Series A to Expand Controlled AI Agent Deployments

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Lumbra announced on November 10, 2026 that it raised a $25 million Series A co-led by Point72 Ventures and Ansa, with continued participation from existing investor Costanoa, to expand its controlled AI-agent platform beyond national security into regulated commercial sectors.

Most of the proceeds will fund engineers and deployment teams pursuing customers in finance, healthcare, law and other regulated industries. Lumbra is also hiring across product and go-to-market roles, including new graduates. The company says its agents already operate in production on top-secret networks across the Pentagon and U.S. Intelligence Community.

LumbraOS is model-neutral, allowing customers to combine frontier-lab models with open-source alternatives. The platform has three components: Nebula manages agent execution, StarChart holds organizational knowledge, and Astrolabe handles evaluation. Nebula links individual claims to their sources, records what agents do and applies data protections. Domain experts must validate results before an agent can write them back to a system of record.

StarChart’s knowledge graph stores insights and conclusions alongside their sources and sensitivity labels. Customers retain the data rights and can export their graphs. Astrolabe evaluates agents ahead of rollout, then continues scoring live systems against custom benchmarks. Each agent accesses information where it is stored and under that source’s permissions, so customer data does not need to be duplicated.

Lumbra’s technology documentation describes evaluation as a continuous process, with every result traceable through its reasoning, underlying documents and the checks it passed. Validation compares outputs with reference material that people specify and maintain, instead of depending only on another model to judge the output. The orchestration around stochastic models is designed to be deterministic, giving oversight teams a repeatable execution path whenever the workflow, context and evaluation criteria are unchanged. The platform supports air-gapped, on-premises and sovereign-cloud deployments, with no data leaving the boundary, audited dependencies and signed, reproducible container images.

Co-founder and CEO Aaron Brown described the intended change in how operators supervise agents: “The organizations that get agents right will spend less time telling them what to do and more time deciding what they may touch and what their work must prove.”

Lumbra reports two early government milestones alongside the round: it signed its first U.S. intelligence-agency contract two months after product work began and moved a later Department of War contract from award to production on top-secret networks in six months.

The Department of War launched Agent Network on June 25, 2026, naming Palantir Technologies and Lumbra in its official release as contributors to a project led by the Chief Digital and Artificial Intelligence Office with U.S. Pacific, Southern and European Commands. The system uses agents to examine defense intelligence and operational systems, turning findings into options for commanders. Target selection and strikes remain human decisions; the system does not take either action autonomously. Testing, operational evaluation and oversight will continue during development and fielding.

Commercial work is already at the engagement stage, according to Lumbra’s domains documentation. Financial-data providers and research teams are working with the company on diligence, sanctions screening and supply-chain risk. Target workflows also include financial research and know-your-customer and anti-money-laundering checks. The clinical work includes active engagements and live surgical-preparation workflows, with revenue-cycle and population-health uses identified as adjacent applications.

The Series A therefore connects Lumbra’s commercial ambitions to a specific test: whether controls built for classified networks can meet the governance demands of regulated buyers while the company adds the engineering and deployment staff needed to support them.

IBM and Microsoft Launch Imagine Enterprise for Industry AI Agents

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

IBM Consulting and Microsoft announced Imagine Enterprise on November 10, 2026, a jointly developed platform for creating, operating and overseeing AI agents tailored to industry processes, drawing on IBM Consulting’s inventory of more than 6,000 pre-built AI agents and assets.

The platform pairs IBM’s industry and delivery expertise with Microsoft’s AI, data, cloud, identity and security technologies. It is designed to connect with an enterprise’s existing data, processes and systems, with the aim of letting organizations adapt pre-built capabilities without replacing their current technology environment or assembling each component themselves.

Forward-deployed engineers from both companies can work with customer teams on qualifying engagements to help move projects from experimentation into production. Clients can engage IBM Consulting to deploy and operate the platform, collaborate with IBM on enterprise-scale implementations, or choose self-management.

Initial industry frameworks cover financial services, insurance and retail. Manufacturing, energy, the public sector and mobility are identified only as possible future additions.

IBM’s launch overview says the companies jointly invested in and co-developed the platform. IBM Consulting Advantage’s Process Studio and Context Studio connect agents to an organization’s own data and processes, while Microsoft Foundry, GitHub Copilot and Copilot Studio help coordinate agents with enterprise systems. Microsoft Fabric, Microsoft 365, Entra, Purview, Defender and Azure provide the surrounding data, identity, governance, security and cloud foundation.

That product division places governance inside the deployment stack rather than leaving it to a separate oversight exercise. For regulated industries, the platform includes controls, observability and AI-compliance support intended to govern agents connected to enterprise knowledge and daily work.

IBM says every agent action, decision and escalation is logged, with real-time data on latency, cost, token usage and run history. Governance controls set which actions an agent may take autonomously, which require human approval and when escalation is mandatory, and agents cannot bypass their defined authority limits.

An operational intelligence layer monitors performance and recommends improvements, with approved changes redeployed automatically. Microsoft Defender, Purview, Entra and Sentinel add real-time visibility over agents in production. The operating model therefore leaves defined approval decisions with people while automating performance monitoring and the redeployment of approved changes.

IBM’s example applies the model to insurance claims, a document-intensive process that runs from first notification of loss through settlement. Based on client interviews, standard operating procedures and industry benchmarks, the process is divided among digital workers with defined responsibilities, controls and escalation paths.

A Digital Steward orchestrates the work. It assesses claim complexity, flags possible fraud signals, checks policy coverage and routes cases according to their characteristics and an adjuster’s authority. Simple claims can go to junior adjusters, coverage ambiguity to senior adjusters, and complex cases with multiple anomaly flags to principal adjusters or specialist investigation units.

The employee receives a prioritized queue explaining why work was routed, what has already been investigated and the recommended next action. The example leaves the claim decision with the human; digital workers handle preparation, triage and follow-through.

SEI, a provider of financial technology, operations and asset-management services, is exploring emerging and agentic technologies with IBM Consulting and Microsoft support. It is also using other technologies from both companies to improve its operations and the experiences it delivers to employees and clients. Its role in the announcement is exploratory, without quantified production results for Imagine Enterprise.

Sneha Shah, SEI’s Chief AI Strategist and Head of SEI Next, said: “Our work with IBM Consulting and Microsoft is helping us explore how emerging capabilities could support critical processes and be responsibly scaled over time, with the goal to create better experiences for our employees and clients.”

The launch connects a large consulting asset base to an extensive enterprise technology stack, but its adoption model depends on more than supplying agents. The initial industry frameworks, approval boundaries and deployment options define a specific execution test: turning pre-built components into governed operations that fit the way individual enterprises already work.

On-Device Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech in Google AI Edge Gallery

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Today, we are introducing on-device Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) and Text-to-Speech (TTS) capabilities in Google AI Edge Gallery. Voice is often the most natural way to interact with AI on phones and wearables. Recent advances in compact speech architectures now make it possible to run accurate, multilingual ASR and expressive TTS models entirely on device, all within a small memory footprint, with low latency, and completely offline. By pairing these lightweight speech models with on-device small language models (SLMs) like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4, developers can build apps that stream live transcriptions as the user speaks and synthesize natural spoken replies locally.

This release brings together major advancements across both Google’s system speech models and the open-weight ecosystem:

Below, we’ll explore how this local voice pipeline powers the new XR (Extended Reality) Language Teacher showcase, upgrades AI Chat in Google AI Edge Gallery, and how you can build with these APIs and models today.

Learning a language while traveling happens in the real world, often in underground stations, busy markets, or abroad where Wi-Fi and roaming data may not be available. To show how vision, language, and speech models can work together 100% locally on your phone with no network connection needed, we built the XR Language Teacher showcase in Google AI Edge Gallery. Simply point your camera at an unfamiliar object or menu, ask "What is that, and how do I ask for one?", and immediately hear an explanation in your language alongside authentic pronunciation in the local language.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

With inference running locally on a paired phone, the same experience works standalone on a phone and extends naturally to hands-free smart glasses. To see how we adapted Language Teacher for intelligent glasses check out our recent talk at Android Dev Summit: Building Audio-First Experiences for Intelligent Eyewear with Gemini Nano.

Beyond the XR Language Teacher showcase, we also built ASR and TTS directly into Google AI Edge Gallery’s main AI Chat feature, giving on-device SLMs like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4 a natural, offline voice interface while on the go.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

While multimodal models like Gemma 4 can process audio inputs directly, we provide a modular solution for cascading language models with separate, lightweight voice models for latency optimization and flexibility. Depending on your target devices and model requirements, our open-source code on GitHub shows you the optimized and recommended solution for Android using ML Kit, as well as MediaPipe Tasks and LiteRT for cross-platform coverage and open model support.

For developers building for Android devices, ML Kit’s GenAI Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech APIs provide a standardized, platform-integrated path out of the box. With the underlying speech models managed through AICore and Google’s on-device speech services, developers get production-grade quality without having to manage model downloads or worry about APK size bloat. Your app also benefits automatically from hardware acceleration across NPU and TPU backends (when available) and seamless background model updates.

Integrating ASR and TTS into cross-platform applications usually requires handling low-level audio framing, Voice Activity Detection (VAD), and complex decoding pipelines. MediaPipe Task simplifies this by providing unified, turnkey ASR and TTS APIs across iOS, macOS, Windows, Linux, and Web that abstract away manual pre-processing, audio stream routing, and platform-specific audio management into simple, high-level interfaces:

Under the hood, ML Kit and MediaPipe Tasks are powered by LiteRT, Google’s high-performance on-device AI inference infrastructure. Cross-platform applications requiring even more fine-grained control over speech processing or custom model architectures can also build on it directly.

Speech models often rely on multiple subgraphs and auxiliary assets, such as encoder/decoder subgraphs and tokenizers for ASR, or phonemizer dictionaries, voice embeddings, and vocoders for TTS. All required components for each model are packaged into a single .litertlm file for easy deployment. The app ships with ready-to-use .litertlm models, including Parakeet-TDT-0.6B-v3 (multilingual ASR), PocketTTS 100M (multilingual TTS), Phonon 31M (ultra-lightweight English TTS), and Kokoro-82M (multilingual TTS). Beyond these, the vibrant Hugging Face LiteRT Community with over 10K+ followers offers a growing catalog of open-weight models, like Whisper, Qwen3-ASR and Moonshine for ASR, or Qwen3-TTS and Matcha for TTS. For developers seeking custom implementations or support for additional open-weight voice models, LiteRT provides a rich set of multimodal model examples to help you get started, with more on the way.

Adding a local voice pipeline takes on-device SLMs beyond typing-based chat interfaces, enabling natural, voice-first interactions that work anywhere, completely offline. All the building blocks to try these experiences or integrate them into your own apps are available today:

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Can we comfortably retire with $5.45M invested and $185K annual spending?

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

It may be pessimistic regarding what will happen. But historically, I think it is realistic about what could happen.

The 4% guideline is based on withdrawal rates that had some failures (historically) by year 30. Withdrawal rates greater than 4% had some failures. Withdrawal rates under 4% did not have failures.

Unfortunately, we don't have any data for 40 year withdrawal rates, but people tend to believe that the rate needs to be less than 4% to be comfortable calling it "safe".

Depending on what the taxes actually are (I estimated), this poster is very near or at 4%. That may (or may not) be too high for a 40 year retirement. We don't know yet because that 40 years has not happened.
Sure, but the 4% rule also requires that they blindly withdraw whatever the calculation says they should each and every year regardless of how their timeline plays out. In practice nobody would actually do that. 185k annual spend should leave plenty of fat to trim should they choose to.

Google Fi announces super network automatic carrier switching for improved speed and reception

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It’s rolling out first on Pixel phones before wider availability arrives in 2027.

Getting the fastest mobile data speeds can be tricky because a single carrier may not have optimal coverage in your area. So to address this, Google Fi is introducing its new super network feature which allows your phone to automatically switch between two different carriers to deliver the best connection possible.

The super network feature begins rolling out today to customers with Pixel phones (Pixel 9 and above with eSIMs). In the event that your phone detects that your connection is sluggish, it will automatically switch from one carrier to another. Unfortunately, Google Fi is not officially disclosing the names of the carriers it's using for its super network, though based on previous partnerships one of them is probably T-Mobile. Regardless, the effect is that if you hit a dead zone or congestion, your phone can connect to a different cell signal without any manual input.

However, that's not all, because alongside the super network, Google Fi is also introducing a new Wi-Fi Auto-Connect+ feature designed to improve data speeds indoors in places like malls, airports and more. That means even if you're in a building that interferes with traditional cellular reception, your phone can switch over to Wi-Fi where available, while automatic encryption ensures that your data stays secure. Wi-Fi Auto-Connect+ will initially be available on Pixel phones (Pixel 5a and newer) and some Motorola and Samsung phones (running OneUI 9 and above) across select locations throughout the US, Europe, Asia and Brazil.

But perhaps the best part is that both Google Fi's super network and Wi-Fi Auto Connect+ will be available to customers with Unlimited Premium and Flexible plans at no extra cost. Furthermore, for international travelers, both features will work in over 200 destinations across the globe.

Alternatively, for customers who need a little extra help or technical support, the company is rolling out its new AI-powered Ask Fi agent inside the app. It's designed to answer questions, straighten out billing concerns or help troubleshoot things like connectivity issues in real time. Thankfully, Google Fi says there will still be actual humans you can reach out to if that's what you prefer.

Anthropic takes on Google and Microsoft with Claude Sheets

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The company also has a new whiteboard app.

Anthropic is significantly expanding Claude's suite of productivity tools. At an event in San Francisco today, the company announced Claude Sheets. As you can probably guess, Sheets allows Claude to generate and edit spreadsheets for you. Each file can include its own set of tabs and formulas. You'll also find the usual assortment of formatting options.

Alongside Claude Sheets, Anthropic announced two other new products: Claude Meetings and Claude Whiteboards. The first of those is Anthropic's take on a meeting agent like the kind offered by Otter.ai and other companies. Available in Google Meet to start (which, incidentally, offers its own meeting agent through Gemini), Claude can join your video calls and start taking notes as you and your colleagues talk. You can later chat with Claude about any plans or action items that came up during your call. "Claude marks itself as a participant and posts a notice in the meeting's chat," says Anthropic. "It won't join a call with outside guests unless an admin allows it, and it leaves if one joins anyway." At the moment, Claude Meetings is only available to Team and Enterprise customers.

Moving to Claude Whiteboards, it's a pretty cutesy take on a collaborative brainstorming app. As your team works together, Claude will group everyone's ideas together by theme and draft action items for each individual. In a nifty bit of integration, you can use Claude Meetings and Whiteboards at the same time. Anthropic is billing the tool as a natural extension of how people already use Claude. "Many people already brainstorm with Claude, but sharing the results with coworkers has meant copy-pasting them somewhere else," the company says. Claude Whiteboards is available in beta to all paid users across the web, desktop and mobile.

Separately from those announcements, Anthropic is renaming Claude Dashboards to Claude Data and starting to roll out the tool to all paid users. Anthropic says it decided to rename the app because "companies use their data for more than dashboards." In any case, you can connect Claude Data to your company's CRM so that the chatbot can then answer specific questions you might have about what's inside.

Lastly, Anthropic is upgrading Claude's plugin functionality, which allows you to connect apps like Canva and Notion to the chatbot so it can pull information from those services. As Anthropic notes, there are already more than 9,000 connectors offered through the Claude Marketplace, but now you can open full apps inside of Claude, allowing the chatbot to act on what it sees in the window. Anthropic is rolling out this update starting with a small group of partners that includes Adobe, Atlassian and HubSpot.

Is it? Vanguard's forecast average annual return numbers are all nominal returns, not real returns.

If we assume 3% / yr CPI inflation, lower than inflation today & a little lower than US long term averages, then Vanguard's forecast nominal returns for global ex US equities of 5% / yr -- 7% / yr end up being a range of 2% / yr -- 4% / yr after subtracting 3% / yr inflation.

At the upper end of Vanguard's forecast band, it agrees with your 4% / yr real assumption. At the lower end of Vanguard's forecast band, you're only getting half of your assumed real return.
There are inflation estimates in their tables too.

The median was 2%, not 3%

I’m not saying the future will be exactly the median forecast, but I do feel like 4% real return over 20-30 years is a pretty reasonable expectation for stocks.

Norovirus on cruise ships: 10 reasons the virus moves so fast at sea

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Norovirus on cruise ships spreads easily through a tiny infectious dose, shared surfaces and close quarters — 10 things to know about outbreaks, tracking and how travelers can stay healthy

Norovirus does not need much help to make people sick. A trace of it on a fingertip, a fork or a stair railing can be enough to start an infection. Put that virus on a ship where passengers and crew share meals, hallways, elevators and entertainment for days. Each new case then gets many chances to become the next one.

That combination explains why norovirus on cruise ships draws so much attention. The virus causes sudden vomiting and diarrhea, and it moves through several routes at once. It travels on hands and shared objects. It can ride along in food and drinks. It also passes through close contact with someone who is sick.

A ship's population also changes with every voyage. New passengers arrive from many different places, while the crew stays aboard as guests come and go. Anyone can carry the virus on board without knowing it.

Cruise outbreaks are also unusually visible. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention runs a dedicated sanitation program for many cruise ships that call at U.S. ports. Those ships report gastrointestinal illness to the agency. The CDC publicly lists voyages whose illness counts cross its outbreak threshold. Most U.S. norovirus outbreaks happen elsewhere, in settings such as long-term care facilities. Those outbreaks rarely draw the same public scrutiny.

For travelers, the useful question is less whether norovirus exists at sea and more how it moves and what slows it down. Some familiar habits offer less protection than people assume. A clear picture of the virus's routes makes it easier to judge which precautions address which risks.

The 10 slides that follow break down the factors behind shipboard spread. They cover the biology of the virus, the layout and routines of a cruise ship and the way illness can board on embarkation day. They explain how long someone stays contagious, why hand hygiene method matters and how the CDC tracks outbreaks at sea. The final slides describe how crews respond when cases appear and how passengers can recover safely.

Norovirus needs very little to take hold. The CDC says as few as 18 virus particles can infect a person, an amount far too small to see, smell or taste.

That number is the starting point for understanding how the virus moves through a cruise ship. Infection does not require a large or obvious exposure. Contamination invisible to the eye can carry more than enough virus to cause illness.

The math works against prevention from the other side as well. The CDC notes that people sick with norovirus can shed billions of virus particles in their stool and vomit. One ill person can release far more virus than it takes to infect someone else. A small, unnoticed lapse in cleanup or handwashing can leave behind many times the infectious dose.

This gap between what a sick person releases and what a healthy person needs turns single cases into chains of infection. Each new case becomes another source of virus. On land, people disperse after an exposure to separate homes, offices and schools. On a ship, the same group of people stays together until the voyage ends. That gives each chain more chances to continue.

The low dose also explains why partial measures can fall short. A cleaning routine that removes most contamination may still leave enough behind to infect someone. Reducing the amount of virus on a hand or surface is not the same as removing it.

The CDC identifies norovirus as the leading cause of vomiting and diarrhea in the U.S. It is also the leading cause of foodborne illness in the country. Both rankings reflect the same trait. A virus that infects so readily has many ways to reach new hosts.

For cruise passengers, the practical lesson is that norovirus does not need a dramatic contamination event to spread. Routine contact with a small amount of virus is often enough. That is why the habits that limit exposure matter even when no one nearby appears sick.

Norovirus passes easily from person to person, and a cruise ship keeps passengers and crew in close contact in dining rooms, theaters, elevators and corridors for the length of a voyage. That repeated contact gives a single infection many chances to reach other people on board.

The CDC lists close contact with an infected person as one of the main ways norovirus spreads. Its examples include caring for someone who is sick and sharing food or eating utensils with them. On a ship, those situations play out among travel companions who share a cabin, a dinner table or a plate of appetizers.

Shared spaces also bring strangers into contact. Passengers line up for shows, sit close together in theaters, crowd into elevators between decks and gather in lounges and around pools. Shore excursions often put groups on the same buses and tender boats. Each gathering mixes people who would not otherwise meet.

The crew forms its own close community. Crew members typically share cabins, eat in common crew dining areas and work long shifts side by side. Many of them also serve food, clean cabins and staff guest areas. Their daily work connects the crew's living quarters with the passengers' spaces.

A ship is also a closed setting. On land, a person who feels unwell can go home and keep away from coworkers, classmates and strangers. At sea, a sick passenger stays inside the same community until the voyage ends. So does everyone that passenger spent time with in the days before.

Time adds to the risk. A multiday itinerary means the same group of people meets again and again — at breakfast, during activities and at dinner. An infection that starts early in a trip has days of ongoing contact in which to reach new people. Those new cases can in turn infect others while the voyage is still underway.

The result is a population that is dense, mixed and confined for the length of the trip.

Norovirus can stay infectious on hard surfaces, so a railing, elevator button or serving spoon can pass the virus from one passenger to the next. A person who touches a contaminated spot and then touches their mouth can become infected without ever being near a sick person.

That indirect route matters on a ship because so many hands land on the same objects. Passengers grip stairwell and deck railings to steady themselves as the vessel moves. Elevator call buttons and corridor door handles get pressed and pulled again and again over the course of a day.

Buffet utensils carry a similar risk. Tongs, ladles and serving spoons pass through a long line of hands during a single meal service. Each diner who picks one up touches the handle the previous diner just held. Many then go straight to eating.

Bathroom fixtures add another set of contact points. Faucet handles, flush levers, stall locks and exit doors are touched by people right after using the toilet. Norovirus is shed in stool and vomit. Hands that were not washed thoroughly can carry it onto the next surface they touch.

The infected person does not need to be present for this kind of spread. Particles left on a surface can be picked up by someone who arrives later. That gap in time and place makes the source hard to trace. Two passengers who never meet can share an illness through a single handrail.

Contaminated hands can also move the virus onward to new objects. A passenger who presses a contaminated button can transfer particles to a menu, a deck chair or a cabin key card.

Hand-to-mouth contact completes the route. People touch their faces often without noticing it. Eating, drinking and biting a fingernail all bring fingers to the lips. On a cruise, shared meals and snacks create many of those moments each day.

Norovirus spreads through food and drinks that an infected person has handled or that were contaminated before they reached the ship. On a cruise, large numbers of people eat from the same kitchens. A single lapse can reach far more diners than it would at a typical restaurant.

The CDC identifies norovirus as the leading cause of foodborne illness in the U.S. The virus does not need to multiply in food the way some bacteria do. It only needs to be present.

Contamination usually happens in one of two ways. The first is handling. A cook, server or bartender who is infected can transfer the virus to anything they touch. The risk is highest with food that will not be cooked afterward. Salads, sliced fruit, sandwiches, garnishes and desserts fall into this group. So does ice, which is scooped and served without any heating step.

The second route is contamination at the source. Some foods carry the virus before anyone on board touches them. Oysters and other shellfish harvested from polluted water are a common example. They are often eaten raw. Fresh produce can also pick up the virus during growing, harvesting or packing.

A ship's galley feeds passengers and crew across several dining rooms, cafes and bars. Much of that work draws on shared ingredients and shared prep areas. One contaminated batch or one ill food worker can therefore reach diners in very different parts of the vessel.

Drinks can carry the virus the same way. Drinking water and hand-mixed beverages can become contaminated at the source or by the person preparing them.

Food-related exposure is hard to separate from other routes. A passenger who falls ill after a meal may have been infected by the food itself. The cause could also be a person at the next table or something touched on the way in. That overlap makes it difficult to pin an outbreak at sea on a single source.

People with norovirus are most contagious while they are sick and during the first few days after they recover. The CDC says they can keep shedding the virus for two weeks or more after symptoms end. They can also spread it before they feel ill at all.

That timeline is a large part of why the virus is hard to contain in any group setting. A person stays contagious for longer than they feel sick. The people who pose a risk to others are not always the ones who look unwell.

The illness itself is short. Typical symptoms are vomiting, diarrhea, nausea and stomach pain. They usually appear 12 to 48 hours after exposure and tend to last one to three days.

The contagious window is a different matter. It can open before the first wave of nausea. Someone who was exposed may pass the virus along while they still feel fine. During that stretch, they have no reason to avoid shared spaces or to take extra care with handwashing.

Once symptoms start, a person is at their most infectious. Vomiting and diarrhea put large amounts of virus into the surrounding environment. Bathrooms, hands and anything those hands touch afterward can all become contaminated.

Recovery does not end the risk. A person who feels back to normal may return to eating with others, using shared restrooms and handling common items. The first days after recovery remain a period of high contagiousness, according to the CDC.

The tail end of shedding stretches much longer. That later shedding can continue well after a one- to three-day illness has passed. A person who has fully recovered can still carry the virus on their hands if they skip thorough washing after using the toilet.

On a ship, this timeline plays out in confined, shared quarters. A passenger can be contagious before they know they are sick. They are most infectious while unwell in their cabin. They may still be shedding the virus after they rejoin the dining room and other public areas.

For travelers, feeling better is not the same as being clear of the virus. Careful hand hygiene still matters for days after symptoms fade, not only while someone is visibly ill.

Norovirus usually reaches a cruise ship with the people who board it. A single passenger or crew member who walks up the gangway already infected can introduce the virus to a ship that was clean when the voyage began.

Embarkation day gathers people from many cities, states and countries into one terminal and one vessel. Each traveler arrives from a different community. Each brings whatever illnesses are circulating at home, at the airport or at the hotel where they stayed the night before.

Norovirus is common on land. The CDC identifies it as the leading cause of acute gastroenteritis in the U.S. Most U.S. norovirus outbreaks happen between November and April, according to the CDC. That means a winter or early spring sailing draws passengers from communities where the virus is more active.

Timing makes the problem harder to catch at the pier. Norovirus has an incubation period of 12 to 48 hours. A person exposed the day before departure can pass through check-in feeling well. Symptoms may not appear until the ship is already at sea.

By then, the infected traveler has spent hours in the terminal, in dining areas and in shared hallways. Anyone exposed during that time can fall ill a day or two later, while the ship is still sailing. That timeline is why illness on a cruise can first surface after departure rather than at boarding.

Crew members add a second path. Many crew members stay aboard across several consecutive voyages while passenger groups change around them. An infected crew member can carry the virus from one sailing into the next and expose an entirely new group of travelers.

The changeover between voyages also matters. The gap between one group leaving and the next arriving is short. That leaves limited time to clean cabins, restrooms and public areas thoroughly. Virus left on a surface that cleaners missed can reach the next group of passengers.

A single episode of vomiting can release norovirus particles into the air and scatter them across nearby surfaces. The CDC lists this among the ways the virus spreads. On a cruise ship, it can expose people who never touched the sick person or anything that person handled.

Norovirus illness often begins with sudden, forceful vomiting. That abrupt onset means a passenger may have no time to reach a bathroom. An episode can happen in a dining room, a theater line, an elevator or on a pool deck. Each of those spaces holds people who may be standing or sitting close by.

The particles do not stay in one spot. Some become airborne as tiny droplets that people nearby can swallow. Others settle onto tables, chairs, railings, floors and carpets around the incident. Anyone who later touches those surfaces and then their mouth faces a second round of exposure. That risk remains after the sick person has left the area.

Cabins carry their own version of this risk. A cabin bathroom is a small, enclosed room shared by everyone staying in that cabin. When one occupant vomits there, the toilet, sink, faucet handles, door handle and towels can all pick up the virus. Cabinmates then use the same fixtures throughout the day.

Bedding and clothing are another pathway. The CDC advises removing and washing any clothes or linens that may be soiled with vomit right away. It recommends handling those items carefully, without shaking them, and wearing rubber or disposable gloves while doing so. The CDC also calls for washing them with detergent and hot water at the longest available cycle. Machine drying on the highest heat setting should follow. Hands should be washed afterward.

For passengers, the practical response is distance. Moving away from a vomiting incident in a public area lowers the chance of swallowing airborne droplets. It also reduces contact with surfaces that may have been splashed. Inside a cabin, not sharing towels with a sick cabinmate removes one easy route for the virus to pass from one person to the next.

The CDC's main hand-hygiene advice against norovirus is to wash with soap and running water. The agency says alcohol-based hand sanitizers can be used in addition to handwashing, but they are not a substitute for it.

The gap comes down to how the virus is built. Norovirus is a non-enveloped virus. It lacks the fatty outer layer that alcohol breaks apart in viruses such as influenza and the coronavirus that causes COVID-19. Without that layer to attack, alcohol inactivates norovirus less reliably.

Handwashing works by a different mechanism. Lathering, scrubbing and rinsing physically lift virus particles off the skin and carry them down the drain. The CDC's general handwashing guidance calls for scrubbing for at least 20 seconds. That includes the backs of the hands, the spaces between fingers and the skin under the nails.

Sanitizer has a second weakness. The CDC notes that it may not work well when hands are visibly dirty or greasy. A quick pump after a buffet meal or a deck-chair snack can leave food residue on the skin, along with any virus mixed into it.

The CDC singles out two moments for handwashing: after using the toilet and before eating. Infected people can shed billions of norovirus particles in their stool and vomit, according to the CDC. Washing between the bathroom and the table cuts off a direct path from one person's illness to another person's mouth. On a cruise ship, meals and snacks come many times a day. Each one is another point where clean hands matter.

The same distinction applies to surfaces. For disinfecting contaminated areas, the CDC recommends chlorine bleach solutions at concentrations of 1,000 to 5,000 parts per million.

The U.S. Environmental Protection Agency keeps a list of registered disinfectants, known as List G, whose labels claim effectiveness against norovirus. A product that removes visible grime does not necessarily carry that claim, and it may leave the virus intact.

For passengers, the sanitizer dispenser at a restaurant entrance works best as a backup when no sink is nearby. Soap and water before meals and after bathroom visits remains the CDC's preferred method.

The CDC Vessel Sanitation Program monitors gastrointestinal illness on cruise ships. It publicly lists a voyage as an outbreak once 3% or more of passengers or crew report illness.

The program has a defined scope. It covers ships with foreign itineraries that carry 13 or more passengers and call at U.S. ports. A ship that never calls at a U.S. port falls outside it.

Reporting begins before a ship docks. Covered vessels send the CDC counts of gastrointestinal illness among passengers and crew ahead of their arrival at a U.S. port. Health officials receive the numbers while the voyage is still under way. That timing lets the agency see a problem before passengers walk off the ship.

The 3% threshold is what moves a cluster of sick travelers onto the CDC's public outbreak list. The bar can be met by passengers or by crew. On a voyage carrying 2,000 passengers, 60 ill passengers would reach it. In a crew of 1,000, 30 sick crew members would do the same. A smaller number of cases still counts as illness, but it does not trigger a public outbreak posting.

Inspections are the program's second tool. Ships receive sanitation inspection scores out of 100, and a score of 86 or higher is satisfactory. The score reflects conditions on the day inspectors visit. It measures something different from the illness counts a ship reports on each voyage. A ship can pass inspection and still have an outbreak on a later voyage.

The program's reporting rules also shape how often cruise outbreaks make news. Every covered voyage feeds into that system, and outbreaks that cross the threshold are posted publicly. Cruise ships account for only a small share of norovirus illness in the U.S. Their outbreaks surface quickly because the reporting system is built to catch them.

Travelers $TRV can check the records themselves. The CDC posts its outbreak list and ship inspection scores online. Anyone can look up a vessel's recent sanitation history before booking a cruise.

When norovirus breaks out on a cruise, ships typically ask sick passengers to stay in their cabins and step up disinfection across the vessel. Travelers $TRV can help contain it by reporting symptoms quickly, keeping hands away from the face and staying apart from others until at least 48 hours after symptoms end.

Cabin isolation works on a simple principle. A sick passenger who stays out of dining rooms, lounges and theaters cannot leave the virus on shared seats, tables or railings. Isolation also keeps that person out of the crowded lines and close conversations where person-to-person spread happens.

Stepped-up disinfection targets the other half of the problem. The work concentrates on the places many hands touch over the course of a day at sea. The aim is to remove virus that has already left an infected person before someone else picks it up.

Prompt reporting matters because a ship can only respond to illness it knows about. A passenger who tells the onboard medical staff about vomiting or diarrhea early gives the crew time to isolate the case. It also lets the crew clean the affected areas sooner. A passenger who hides symptoms keeps moving through shared spaces while contagious.

The 48-hour benchmark comes from CDC guidance on staying away from others after recovery. On a ship, following it can mean skipping excursions, shows and group meals even after a passenger feels better. Feeling well again is not the same as no longer being contagious.

Healthy passengers have their own part to play. Washing with soap and water before meals lowers the chance that a stray particle gets inside the body.

Treatment is supportive. There is no approved norovirus vaccine in the U.S. That means there is no shot to get before boarding. Care centers on drinking fluids to replace what the body loses and to prevent dehydration.

The nickname stomach flu causes confusion. Norovirus is not influenza, and a seasonal flu shot offers no protection against it.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Budgeting app you can trust

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

I use Quicken Simplifi. I use it extensively. I have cash flow per account set, budget plans, saved designed reports, etc.

Tested several apps, for several weeks. I realized that the weak link on all these apps is actually their ability to create (and keep) a solid connection to your bank/brokerage.

I have several account with more than 10 institutions (several credit cards) and Quicken Simplifi has been rock solid.

All the others (specially those who rely in Plaid) dropped connections frequently.

Steam Deck performance gets a reality check with Valve's new FPS data

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Benchmark tools for generic gaming rigs could be coming soon.

Since Valve first announced its Steam Deck Verified program back in 2021, players have found that a game’s “Verified” status doesn’t always match how well it actually runs on the handheld. Now, new Steam Deck frame rate data being shared by Valve itself can help players dig deeper into the kinds of performance they can expect from many Steam Deck games.

Valve has been collecting anonymized frame rate data from Steam players who opt in since February. Yesterday evening, the company announced it has started sharing that data on many Steam Store pages as averages and histograms for both the Steam Deck and Steam Deck OLED.

Valve acknowledges that players enabling VSync or frame rate limits can bias the collected data somewhat and that the measurements don’t account for custom in-game settings that Steam Deck users may tinker with to get a game running. Regardless, Valve says it has nonetheless “found that the framerate data consolidates around relatively narrow ranges.”

This kind of data can be extremely useful for distinguishing between the wide range of performance levels that can exist among “Verified” Steam Deck games. Both Cyberpunk 2077 and Skyrim: Special Edition earn the same Steam Deck Verified badge, for instance, but the former sees average Deck frame rates in the 30-40 FPS range, while the latter usually achieves 50-60 FPS on the hardware.

Valve’s frame rate data is even more useful for games that the company insists are “Unsupported” on Steam Deck because their “graphics setting cannot be configured to run well” on the hardware or because Valve is “still working on adding support for this game.” Despite warnings like these, games like Starfield, Silent Hill 2, and Assassin’s Creed Syndicate, to name just a few, all show extremely playable frame rates reported by actual Steam Deck users. Assassin’s Creed Syndicate in particular approaches a 60 FPS average on the Steam Deck OLED hardware despite its “Unsupported” status.

Frame rate data can also be useful for judging the single-player modes of games whose multiplayer doesn’t work under SteamOS. Watch Dogs 2, for example, is officially Unsupported on Steam Deck because of anti-cheat compatibility issues, but Steam Deck players report averages of 40+ FPS on the game’s single-player modes.

Of course, collecting and averaging frame rate data for the Steam Deck(s) is made simpler by their set hardware configuration. When it comes to communicating expected frame rates for the wide range of hardware in more generalized gaming PCs, Valve says it is “always looking for new ways to help developers understand the experiences customers are having with their games.”

Back in April, JSON files buried in the Steam client pointed to a potential Steam “Framerate Estimator” tool that could let users “Select an App and a PC config to get a chart of estimated framerates, based on the framerates of other Steam users,” according to strings contained within. That could be incredibly useful for Steam users who want to go beyond the listed Minimum and Recommended hardware requirements without relying on shady-looking third-party FPS-estimator websites. In the meantime, is sharing average frame rate data for the fixed-configuration Steam Machine too much to ask?

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

DHT Holdings (DHT) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Record tanker rates drove results and a 67th straight dividend payout.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

DHT Holdings, Inc. (DHT +1.22%) reported record financial results for the third quarter of 2026, surpassing the previous record set in the second quarter. Management reported that the performance was driven by a supportive tanker market and strong commercial execution across its fleet. The company maintained its capital allocation policy of returning 100% of ordinary net income to shareholders while expanding its fleet through a newbuilding program and strategic vessel sales. The company stated that ongoing logistical disruptions and shifting crude procurement patterns have increased transportation distances, sustaining elevated freight rates.

Operator: Good day, and thank you for standing by. Welcome to the Q3 2026 DHT Holdings, Inc. Earnings Conference Call. [Operator Instructions] Please be advised that today's conference is being recorded. I would now like to hand the conference over to your first speaker today, Laila Halvorsen, CFO. Please go ahead.

Laila Halvorsen: Thank you. Good morning and good afternoon, everyone. Welcome, and thank you for joining DHT Holdings Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Call. I'm joined by DHT's President and CEO, Svein Moxnes Harfjeld. As usual, we will go through financials and some highlights before we open up for your questions. The link to the slide deck can be found on our website, dhtankers.com. Before we get started with today's call, I would like to make the following remarks. A replay of this conference call will be available on our website, dhtankers.com, until November 17. In addition, our earnings press release will be available on our website and on the SEC EDGAR system as an exhibit to our Form 6-K.

As a reminder, on this conference call, we will discuss matters that are forward-looking in nature. These forward-looking statements are based on our current expectations about future events as detailed in our financial report. Actual results may differ materially from the expectations reflected in these forward-looking statements. We urge you to read our periodic reports available on our website and on the SEC EDGAR system, including the risk factors in these reports for more information regarding risks that we face. As usual, we'll start the presentation with some financial highlights. The third quarter of 2026 was a record quarter for the company, surpassing the previous record achieved in the second quarter of 2026.

The results reflected a supportive tanker market and strong commercial performance. In the third quarter, we achieved revenues on TCE basis of $290 million and adjusted EBITDA of $269.5 million. Net income came in at $270.9 million, equal to $1.68 per share. After adjusting for the noncash fair value gain related to interest rate derivatives of $2.2 million and the gain on sale of DHT Bauhinia of $34.2 million, we had ordinary net income for the quarter of $234.5 million, equal to $1.45 per share. Vessel operating expenses for the quarter were $19 million and G&A for the quarter was $4.3 million.

In terms of market performance, our vessels trading in the spot market earned an average of $167,100 per day, while the vessels on time charters achieved $114,200 per day. The average combined TCE for the fleet in the quarter was $141,300 per day. We continue to maintain a very strong balance sheet, supported by conservative leverage and robust liquidity. At the end of the third quarter, total liquidity was $555 million, consisting of $154.7 million in cash and $400.7 million available under our revolving credit facilities. At quarter end, financial leverage was 10.5% based on market values for the fleet and net debt was about $15 million per vessel, well below estimated residual values.

Looking at our cash flow, we began the quarter with a cash balance of $162 million. During the quarter, operations generated $269.5 million in EBITDA. Debt repayment and cash interest totaled $17 million. Proceeds from sale of vessel was $51 million and $197 million was distributed to shareholders through a cash dividend. In addition, we invested $4.6 million in vessels and $91 million in vessel under construction. Changes in working capital and other items amounted to $93 million, and the quarter ended with $154.7 million in cash.

In line with our capital allocation policy of paying out 100% of ordinary net income as quarterly cash dividends, the Board has approved a dividend of $1.45 per share for the third quarter of '26. This marks our 67th consecutive quarterly cash dividend. The shares will trade ex-dividend on November 23, and the dividend will be paid on November 30 to shareholders of record as of November 23. On the left side of this slide, we also present our estimated P&L and cash breakeven level for next year.

Our P&L breakeven for 2027 is currently estimated at $29,200 per day, while our cash breakeven is currently estimated at $20,600 per day, which reflects all true cash costs, meaning OpEx, G&A, CapEx, debt installments and cash interest. The difference between our P&L and cash breakeven is currently estimated at an average of $8,600 per day per vessel. The discretionary cash flow will remain within the company and be allocated for general corporate purposes. On this slide, we present an update on bookings to date for the fourth quarter of 2026. We expect 1,174 time-charter days covered for the fourth quarter at an average rate of $100,200 per day.

This rate includes profit sharing for the month of October and the base rate only for November and December for contracts with profit sharing features. Two of our vessels operate under time-charter arrangements with profit sharing mechanism, allowing them to participate in the spot market upside. We also anticipate 930 spot days for the quarter, of which 68% or 630 days have been booked at an average rate of $579,900 per day. These bookings reflect a very strong market environment and key up for another quarter with record earnings.

Importantly, none of the booked spot days relate to voyages in the Persian Gulf, demonstrating that earnings have been achieved across our broader trading portfolio and in line with DHT's risk appetite. The first 9 months of 2026 have already exceeded the company's previous record full year financial performance by a good margin. Given the strength of bookings to date with the fourth quarter expecting to surpass the third quarter, 2026 will establish a new annual earnings record for the company. The spot P&L breakeven for the quarter is estimated to be below 0 as earnings from our time-charter portfolio are expected to more than cover forecasted operating, financing and overhead costs.

And now I'll turn the call over to Svein.

Svein Moxnes Harfjeld: Thank you, Laila. I will now briefly walk through our key quarterly highlights. We delivered the DHT Bauhinia, built 2007, to the new owners in July 2026. Cash proceeds from the sale were $51 million, whilst the capital gain was $34.2 million. We took delivery of a newbuilding, the DHT Impala on July 24. This was some 5 months earlier than the contractual commitment by the yard and marks the fourth and final vessel in our 2026 newbuilding program. The vessel entered the spot market with a highly rewarding start to her life with us. We entered into 3-year time-charter contracts for DHT Jaguar, built 2015, and DHT Panther, built 2016, with rates at $75,000 and $100,000 per day, respectively.

The vessels delivered into their services in September and October. We have completed dry dockings for 7 vessels year-to-date. All projects were completed on schedule and within budget, marking the completion of our 2026 drydocking program. We have 4 vessels scheduled for dry docking during 2027. These are DHT Sundarbans, DHT Opal and DHT Taiga, all built in 2012, which will undergo their third special surveys, whereas DHT Tiger, built in 2017, will undergo her second special survey. And now moving to events subsequent to the quarter. In line with our long-term fleet development plans, we have entered into an additional newbuilding contract with Hanwha Ocean.

This will be the sixth addition to the Antelope class, which commenced delivery in January '26. She will be named DHT Sable and will also feature large carrying capacity and come equipped with an exhaust gas cleaning system. She is scheduled for delivery in April '29 and leaves us with an order book of 2 VLCC buildings -- newbuildings, the first scheduled for delivery in August '28. Both orders have been concluded with what should be assumed to be early and competitive delivery slots. These orders will be financed by internal resources, cash flow from operations and projected new mortgage debt. We will share some thoughts on the order book and what it potentially could mean for DHT.

The current order book now stands at 320 ships over the coming 4 years against the fleet of 940 ships. The fleet total includes ships storing oil. We expect 44% of the fleet to be older than 15 years by the end of 2030, 30% older than 20 years and 14% older than 25, hence the global fleet renewal is required. These numbers assume no scrapping. China is developing its shipyard industry at an impressive pace and is set to construct some 77% of the current VLCC order book. Within the Chinese shipyards representing this part of the order book, some have experience in building large tankers and some have ambitious plans for this space.

Looking under the hood of the total order book, we have at our liberty broken it down to 4 categories being; 1, traditional shipowners; 2, oil companies/traders; 3, national companies; and 4, financial actors. We believe parts of the order book to represent ships or fleets that not necessarily have natural homes. We have built up a strong and resourceful balance sheet to support continued investments in our fleet and customer offerings when the right opportunities appear. We think it's reasonable to expect that some of such opportunities could evolve out of the order book in due course, hence the perceived threat could become an opportunity to DHT.

The freight market remains strong due to ongoing logistical disruptions resulting in operational inefficiencies in combination with shifting crude procurement patterns expanding transportation distances. Geopolitical risk persists because recent events demonstrate for the first time in history that the full or partial closure of the Strait of Hormuz is possible, altering long-term risk perceptions even if regular traffic resumes. Supply chain resilience is a priority for Middle Eastern oil producers, but expanding their logistical capabilities will not automatically restore the tanker fleet's historical productivity levels. Energy security concerns should drive a major inventory restocking cycle by governments and corporations, which is expected to boost transportation demand above actual consumption levels.

Private consolidation is tightening market dynamics following a structural fleet consolidation by a private aggregator earlier in the year, resulting in fragmented spot supply and restricted information flows. Limited effective fleet supply growth due to an aging fleet, a growing share of vessels operating outside mainstream trades and a limited near-term newbuilding delivery schedule. News flows and U.S. midterm election results move commodity and capital markets and fuels volatility, including significant near-term deltas between physical and derivative markets for freight as well as creating large bid-ask spreads in term contracts. And with that, operator, we are now ready to open the floor for questions.

Svein Moxnes Harfjeld: Omar, I think the news flow proven this year has been very, very volatile, right? And we had news in one direction one day and news in another direction in the next day and some of the psychology spills into, of course, markets and how they're playing out. I think it's actually very hard to know how this will work now and whether there is a deal or not, right? And -- but I really think that if for sake of argument, there is a deal with some level of opening or resumption of traffic, it will take quite a while to unwind all these inefficiencies that is in place, right?

And -- so I have a hard time to see that anything falling out of bed, frankly. We have ships in the market all the time. And as of present, there is still significant interest at very good levels for vessels that we have available in the spot market. So -- and it will vary a bit from trade to trade. I would say that trades in and around the Middle East are probably holding up a bit better than some of the South American trades. But the market is dynamic. So I think you just need to look at the number, and I think this is good for the shareholders in all simple -- that's my simple answer really.
